# Glossary to the History of Ducas (Ducae Historiam Byzantinam - Glossarium)

**Author**: Doukas  
**Series**: PG 157  
**Columns**: 1199–1268

INDICES
IN
DUCE HISTORIAM BYZANTINAM.
GLOSSARIUM
VOCUM GRECO-BARBARARUM.
Pag. 33. 'Απληκεύειν. Πληκεύειν. Πληκεύων
ἦν. Eruditissimus vir Ismael Bullialdus Ducam per
pocoper τοῦ a scripsisse putat. Constantinus in
Tacl. ἵνα πληκεύῃ ὁ στρατὸς εἰς ἀραιωτέρους τάς
πους. Meursius rescribebat ἵνα ἀπληκεύῃ, ut statim
ibidem scribitur bis aut ter. Leo, Tactic. cap. xuit,
§ 15. Καὶ ἐν ἑτέρῳ πλήκτῳ ἀπελθεῖν. Idem M. legebat, ἀπλήκτῳ. Est autem πληκεύειν, seu ἁπληκεύειν, castra metari, vel divertere. Theophanes
in Heraclio: πλήκευσαν πλησίον Νινευὴ τῆς πόλ
λεως. Vide ad Cedr. et Histor. Ecclesiasticam Anastasii in ἠπλήκευσεν.
Pag. 68. 'Αποκρησάριος. Ως καὶ καθολικὸς ἀπο
κρισιάριος του βασιλέως γενέσθαι. Apocrisiarius.
Vide ad Cedr. Nicet. et Anastas.
Pag. 84. Αρματα. Arma. Auctor Græcub. Νασί
μουσιν τὰ κάτεργα εἰς γῆν, ὁμοίως καὶ τ᾿ ἄλλα μια
κρὰ μεγάλα παραυτα καὶ ναταξερματώσουν καλυπία
τε καὶ ἄρμενα καὶ ἄρματα παντοία. Vide ad Cedr.
Nicetam, Anastas. et Coust. Manassem.
Ρ.8. 171. Αρχιευνούχος. Archieunuchus, primus Eunuchorum.
Pag. 132. "Ασπρον. Asprum. Numi genus argentci. Est enim άσπρο, album. Iudex in lliad.
Greco-Barb. λευκώλενος, ἄσπρες ταῖς ἀγκάλαις.
Dixi ad Nicetam et Anastasium.
Pag. 54 et 58. Αὐθέντης. Αυθεντία. Dominus,
dominatio. Hinc αὐθεντίζειν εἰ αὐθεντεύειν. Index
Græco-Barb. Homerica Iliad. παντάνακτες, οἱ
πάντα ὁρίζοντες, καὶ αὐθεντεύοντες. Ducas pag.
62, 69, 129. Αυθεντίζειν apud Theophanem. Anastasius vertit, præsumptuose gerere, in Histor.
Eccles., atque ita accipiendum videtur αὐθεντικώς
apud Codinum in παρεκβολ. ἔτι δὲ ἑώρα ὁ χυδαίος
δῆμος αὐθεντικῶς εἰσερχομένους ἐν τῷ μεγάλῳ
παλατίῳ τοὺς Λατίνους, καὶ μέγα κακὸν παθεῖν ἐξ
αὐτῶν ὑπώπτευσεν. ldem Anastasius, αὐθεντικώς,
auclorabiliter vertit. Hodie ἀφέντης οι ἀφεντία,
dominatio, apud auctorem Chronici quod subjicitur Duce. Vide ad Cedren. et Nicetam.

Pag. 107. Γαλεώτης. Caliote, Brigantin. Galea minor diminutivum τῆς γαλέας, vel γαλαίας. Nam utroque modo scribitur. Leo, Tact. cap. xix, § 74. Καὶ τὰς γαλέας. In § autem 10 ejusdem capitis, scribitur, γαλαίας. Matthæus Paris. in Henrico I. Navibusque rostratis, quas Caleias appellant. Italicum galea, Hispanicum galera, Gallicum galère. Pag. 111. Γάρδια. Παραφυλακή, Custodia, la Garde. Pag. 36. Γενητζάριδες. Ἀργυρωνήτους σὺν αὐτῷ δούλους οὓς καλοῦσι γενητζάριδας. Servos pecunia ἐμπτος, quos genitzaridas vocant. Qui et γιανητζάριδες. Auctor anonymus : Ἔχει καὶ γιανητζάριδες χιλιάδες δέκα πέντε. Pag. 76. Γενητζέρι. Genitzeri. Pag. 201. Γυρεύειν. Redire. Vide Gl. ad Nicet. et Indicem.

Pag. 56. Δέσποινα. Imperatoris uxor vel mater, ut pag. 8, δεσποίνης Ἄννης. Codinus, de Offic. cap. v, edit. Reg. num. 27, πρὸς τὴν δέσποιναν τὴν μητέρα αὐτοῦ (alii μάναν ἀ) καὶ τὴν δέσποιναν τὴν γυναῖκα. Theophilus, Institut. ii, tit. 6, in fine. Vide ad Nicetam. Nota obiter. μάναν pro matre. Auctor Græco-Barbarus : ἔχασέ μάνα τὸ παιδί, καὶ τὸ παιδὶν τὴν μάναν. Vide ad Cedr. in ea dictione. Pag. 61. Δεφενδεύειν. Defendere. Hinc δεφένδευσις, defensio. Acropolites pag. 6. Vide ad Nicet. Pag. 147. Διακαιρίσιμος. Diacænesimus. Vide Notas, et Jacobum Gretserum iii observ. in Codinum cap. 14. Pag. 30. Δούκας. Dux. Vide ad Cedreuum, et ad Nicet. in κόντος. Pag. 81. Δρεγγάριος. Præfectus classi. Vide ad Cedr. et Constant. Manass. Huic suberant comites. Vide in Κόμης.

Pag. 106. Ἐνορδίνος. Ordine. Eustathius ii. ψ, ἐνορδίνως ἵστατο. Theophanes in Justiniano juniore : διαταράξας τοὺς ὑπὸ τοῦ οἰκείου πατρὸς ἐνορδίνους γεγονότας τύπους, al. ἐνορδίνως. Sic ἐνορδῖνοι δικασταὶ dicti et ἐνορδίνον δικαστήριον, Judices ordinarii, et judicium ordinarium, apud Theodorum Hermopoliten, lib. vii, tit. 2, et apud Constantinum Harmenop. Epitom. lib. i, tit. iv, § 61 et 62. Qui et ὀρδινάριοι δικασταὶ in Novella cui, cap. 5, ἐμβαθμοι, in Basilicis, ut ὀρδινάριος ἀρχων, apud Synesium epistola cxiv. Utrumque autem dictum ἀπὸ τοῦ ὀρδίνου, sive ordine. Opus Apollo ἱερόγλυφ. cap. xciv, al. xc, αὗται γὰρ ἑαυτὰς φυλάττουσι γρηγοροῦσαι κατ᾿ ὄρδινον ἐν πάσῃ τῇ νυκτί. Constantinus in Tacticis : Ἔχοντα ὀρδίνους ἀπὸ δέκα ἐξ ἀνδρῶν. Et paulo post : Καὶ οἱ μὲν ὀρδῖνοι τῶν πεζῶν ἑνὸς ἑκάστου τάγματος, etc. Leo, Tactic. cap. ix, § 55, εἴτε ἐν ὀρδίνῳ, εἴτε ἐν παρατάξει, Eustathius ad ii. α : Ἀπὸ δὲ τοῦ Ὁμηρικοῦ ὀσδλοῦ, καὶ τὸ γραφικὸν σημεῖον μετῆν.κται ὁ ὀδελός, ὃς ἐστι γραμμή τις εὐθεῖα προτιθεμένη μὲν στίχων, ἤτοι ὀρδίνων, ἔξω. Auctor anonymus pœnar. adversus monachos delinquentes, inter scripta Basilii M. repertus : Εἴ τις οὐκ αἴρῃ εὐλογίαν εἰς τὸν ὀρδῖνον αὐτοῦ. Et ibid. : Εἴ τις μεθ᾿ τὰ


[1202] {#pg.157.1202}
τὸν ἀδελφὸν αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τοῦ ὀρδίνου τῆς ὑπηρεσίας. Hine ὀρδιναλῶς. Glossarium ms. ὀρδιναλῶς, ἑξῆς, μετὰ ταῦτα. Εἰ ὀρδινιάζειν, et προορδινεύειν. Leo, Tact. cap. xvii, § 51. Καὶ οὕτως προορδινεύειν χρὴ τὰ βάνδα, vel ὀρδινιάζειν. Alexius Rharturus Doct. ii. Διάταξις περὶ τοῦ οἴκου σου, ὀρδινίασε τὸ σπίτι σου. Auctor Græco-Barbarus : Καὶ τ᾿ ἄλλα τὰ ὀρδινίασε. Corona pretiosa : præparare, νὰ ὀρδινιάσῃ. Index in Il'ad. Græco-Barb. Παρασκευάζει, ὀρδινιάζει. Pag. 145. Ἑνωτικοί. Unitati adhærentes, et pag. eadem, ἑνωτικὴ λειτουργία. Ἕνωσις. Pag. 142.

Campana. Hinc καμπανάριον, pro turri, in qua suspenditur campana. Astrainpsychus, ἐν τῷ Πυθαγορικῷ λαξευτηρίῳ, δηλοῖ εἰς ὁδὸν καὶ μακρὸν καὶ ὑψηλόν τι, ὅμοιον πύργῳ στύλω καμπανάριῳ. Pag. 53. Κάμπος. Campus. Theodorus Lector, Eccles. hist. lib. i : Ἐν ταῖς λιταῖς ταῖς γενομέναις ἐν τῷ κάμπῳ, occurrit sæpe apud eumdem. Alxander monachus, de Inventione S. Crucis : ὤφθη αὐτῷ ἐν κάμπῳ διέγοντι μετὰ τῶν στρατιωτῶν, περὶ μεσημβρίας στηλοειδὴς σταυρός, quæ totidem verbis exstant in Julii Pollucis Chronico hactenus inedito. Theophanes in Justiniano Jun. Τοῦ δὲ λαοῦ σκορπισθέντος ὧ : πρόβατα ἐπὶ τοὺς κάμπους. Index Iliados Græco-Barbaræ : Πεδιάδες, οἱ κάμποι. Item πεδίον, κάμπος. Pag. 181. Καπιτάνευς. Dux. Capitaneus. Concilium Florentinum sess. xxv : Ἔστειλα γὰρ καὶ ἃ τὸν καιρὸν καπετάνιον ἐν τῇ Βενετίᾳ ἑτοίμασι κάτεργα. Conethum Ephesinum act. vii. Magnificentissimus Capitaneus principi et clero scripsit. Pag. 118. Κάρβουνον. Κάρβων in Basilicis, carbo, et δενδροκάρβωνες, Græci ad i. 5, § 2. D. ad exhibendum καρδωνάριος, carbonarius. Heiladius apud Photium ὅτι ἀνθρακεὺς ὁ ἐν τῇ συνηθείᾳ καρδωνάριος. Pag. 233. Καρίνιδας. Advenæ. Pag. 169. Καστέλλιον. Castellum. Anonymus in Romano imp. Ἐν ἑτέρῳ ὀχυρωτάτῳ καστιλλίῳ. Hinc καστελλᾶν. Theophanes in Phoca : Φέρων πλῆθος καστελλᾶμε ἔχοντα ἐν τῷ καταρτίοις κήρυκα καὶ εἰκόνας τῆς θεομήτορος. Vide alia Glossaria. Pag. 40. Καστελλάρια. Regio. Pag. 40. Κάστρον. Oppidum. Auctor anonymus in Vita Basilii imperatoris, Κατὰ τοῦ κάστρου Εὐρίπου ἐπλήζεται. Cedrenus de eodem pag. 580. Τῇ πόλει ἐπιτίθεται τοῦ Εὐρίπου. Hinc καστρομεγία, civitatum expugnatio. Vide ad Cedrenum, N.cetam, et Indicem Gloss. Acastas, Historke. Pag. 202. Κάτεργα. Triremes. Concilium Florentinum pag. 567, edit. German. Auctor Græco-


[1203] {#pg.157.1203}
Barb. Νασύρουσιν τὰ κάτεργα εἰς γῆν. Vide sup. in καπιτάνεος, et Gloss. ad Nicetam. Pag. 142. Κέλλη. Cella monachi. Anonymus in Constantino Leonis F. Μόλις διελθὼν ὁ ἄναξ ταῖς κέλλαις τῶν ἱερῶν πατέρων καταλάβων. Εἰ : πρὸς τὰς κέλλας τῶν ἱερῶν πατέρων ὑπενόστησεν. Pag. 145. Κελλίον. Cellula. S. Athanas. in Serm. de imag. D. nostri Jesu Christi. Refertur autem Synod. Nicæn. II, act. 4: Χριστιανός τις ἔλαβεν ἐν οἴκῳ κελλίῳ παρά τινος. Theodorus Studita in Testamento suo nondum Græce edito: οὗ κτήσῃ μαθητὴν εἰς τὸ κελλίον σου μειράκιον προσαπῶς. Anonymus Scriptor in Leone imp. Ὁ δὲ Σαμονᾶς : εἰ βούλει πληροφορηθῆναι, φησὶν, οὓς βούλει, εἰς τὸ ἐμὸν κελλίον ἀπόστειλον. Adde Novellam cxlιπ, cap. 1, et Glycam, pag. 287, edit. Reg. Non solum autem pro monachi cellula κελλίον accipitur, sed et pro relicula, vel cubiculo interiore etiam principum. Palladas ἀνθολ. lib. II, tit. xlix, epigrammat, 7: Τῷ πτισάνην πωλοῦντι τὸ κελλίον ἐχθὲς ἔδωκα. Hinc κελλιῶται pro monachis, et pro ministris interioris cubiculi apud Chalcocondyl. pag. 247, et Phranzen. De his etiam Nicetas pag. 326, Vide ibid. Glossar, in ν. κελλίον. Videsis etiam Glossar. ad Cedren. in eadem voce. In ms. tamen cod. Chalcoc. κελλωτῶν legitur. Pag. 47. Κλεισούρα. Clusura. Joannes Europaγates pag. 844. Διὰ τῆς κλεισούρας τοῦ Ποδανοῦ, ἀφικνεῖται εἰς Κιλικίαν. Codinus in Παρεκβολαῖς, ἐπετυφλώθη ἐν τῇ κλεισούρᾳ τῆς μάχης. Vide ad Cedrenum, Nicetam et Anastasium. Pag. 291. Κλητοί. Evocati. In ora Regii codicis, Pregati. Pag. 87. Κόμης. Comes. Suberat drungario. Qui vulgo hodie comite dicitur, a Provincialibus autem comé, multum distat ab hoc comite; hic enim tribus aut quinque triremibus præficiebatur, earumque dux erat, ut scribit Leo imperat. Tact. cap. xix, § 23. Ille vero remis intentus est, remigio præest, curatque ut remiges remos impellant pront res exigit, vel etiam ut vela deducant, aut erigant. Denique signo dato præcipit quemadmodum remos agant, et segnius remis incumbentes coercet. Nec etiam huc pertinet πρωτοκόμης, qui cæteris comětibus præerat; nam hodie singuli comites singulis triremibus præficiuntur, nec alter alteri præest. Habet tamen comes qui vicem ejus expleat, si ipse absit, quem nos subcomitem appellamus, soutocomé. Vide Codinum de Offic. cap. 5, num. 93, et Glossarium ad Nicetam. Pag. 198. Κουρεύειν. Excurrere. Pag. 106. Κουρσεύειν. Prædari. Hinc κοῦρσον, pro incursione et præda. Continuator Theophanis in Theopbilo : Τὰ μὲν τῶν Σαρρακηνῶν κοῦρσα ἐπ᾿ ἄλλον δή τινα τόπον ἐξελαύνειν ποιήσας. Alius auctor in Vita Basilii imperat. κοῦρσον κατὰ τῆς λεγομένης Ζαπέτρας ἀπέστειλεν ἐπιλέκτων πολεμιστῶν. Scriptor anonymus in Romano Constantini F. ἐλάσει κοῦρσα, καὶ αἰχμαλωτίσαι, καὶ παιδεῦσαι τὴν ῥωμαϊκὴν γῆν. Vide ad Cedr. et Nicetam. Pag. 12. Κράλης. Rex Ungaris, Servis. Joannes Cantacuzenus libro iii, cap. 89, Τῷ δὲ υἱῷ τὴν τοῦ Κράλη παρέλχετο προδηγορίαν. Vide Pandecten Turcicum Joannis Leunclavii cap. 51. Pag. 198. Κύρις. Dominus. Vide Glossarium ad Cedr. et Anastasium. Pag. 198. Κῦρος. Dominus. Exstat apud Chalcoc. pag. 264, qui κῦρ, Chir, apud Leonardum Calensem. Hodie etiam κῦρος dicunt.

Μετάζειν, Β-ζύρης. Consiliarius, Vide Not. ad pag. lxxxviii. Auctor Græco-Barbarus in historica narratione de Belisario : Βουλώνουν οἱ μετάζοντες, καὶ ὁ βασιλεὺς ὁ μέγας, id est, ut videtur, consiliarii principis. Habet et alias significationes, aut certe varia ejus ministeria. Joannes Cantacuzenus, Histor. lib. iii. cap. 36, βασιλίδι ἦν, Vertunt : Imperatricis aulæ præfectus, sive curator. Hinc μετάζειν, lib. ejusdem cap. 15, τὴν μὲν τοῦ μετάζειν ὑπηρεσίαν ἐχειρίσθη. Officium administratoris obtinuit. Eumdem esse putant cum eo quem JC. Comitem rerum privatarum appellant. Vide adnotationes eruditissimi viri Jacobi Pontani ad lib. i, cap. 2. Corrigendus hic est Nicetas Manuelis Comneni lib. 1, num. 3, pag. 37 edit. Reg. ubi legitur, Μαλεζωνῶν δὲ μεσεύοντα καὶ τῶν οἰκείων ὑποδραστήσα διαταγμάτων. Puto enim Nicetam scripsisse : Μαλεδ. δὲ τῶν, etc., et illud, μεσεύοντα, irrepsisse in contextum cum interpretationis vice, vel ex codice Nicetæ GræcoBarbaro in ora ascriptum esse. In codice enim Græco-Barbaro sic habetur : Μετάζοντα δὲ τῶν οἰκείων ὑποθέσεων. Ὑποθέσεις igitur sunt διατάγματα et ita ea vox accipienda apud Codin. de Offic. cap. 1, num. 9. In Indice Nicetæ, μετάζων exponitur, φροντιστής. At in Glossario ita scribendum : Μετάζων, μελεδωνὸς καὶ τῶν οἰκείων ὑποδραστήρ διαταγμάτων. Vide ad Codin. de Officiis. Pag. 142. Μονάζουσαι. Sic etiam scribitur in Actis sanctorum martyrum Thraciæ, Probi, et Andronici; et in passione Abundii et Abundantii, Græci σπεκουλάτωρ scribunt, ut in Evangelio D. Marci cap. vi, vers. 27, qui spiculator veteri interpreti. S. Athanasius, Apol. ii: Καὶ παρῆν σπεκουλάτωρ. Glossæ ms. σπεκουλάτωρ, ἀποκεφαλιστής. In Glossis tamen Græco-Lat. scribitur, speculator, et exponitur, δήμιος, ut a Dicne, et ἀποκεφαλίζων, quod scilicet carnificis officium perageret. Et in Suetonii Augusto, cap. 27, et in t. Divus 6 D. De bon. damnat. qui Basil. lib. lx, tit. 52, ὁ ἀποτέμνων. Verum ipsi etiam satellites, seu δορυφόροι, spiculatores dicuntur in veteri Onomastico. Itaque spiculatores qui dicunt, eos a spiculis dictos existimant. Qui διοπτῆρας, vel διόπτας καὶ ἐρευνητὰς vertunt, omnino speculatores intelligunt. Certe Cujacius ἀποτέμνοντα in d. tit. 52, spiculatorem vertit, et Bonaventura Vulcanius in Notis ad Glossarium Latino-Græc. scribit, inepte ibi confundi speculatorem cum spiculatore. PATHOL. GR. CLVII.
INDICES TO DUCAS' BYZANTINE HISTORY. GLOSSARY OF GRECO-BARBAROUS WORDS.
Page 33. 'Απληκεύειν (Aplekeuein). Πληκεύειν, Πληκεύων ἦν. The most learned Ismael Bullialdus thinks that Ducas wrote it with an "o" (ποκοπερ τοῦ). Constantine in his *Tactics*: "lest the army should encamp (πληκεύῃ) in more sparse locations." Meursius emended it to "lest it should encamp" (ἵνα ἀπληκεύῃ), as it is written there twice or three times immediately following. Leo, *Tactica*, ch. XIII, § 15: "And to depart into another encampment (πλήκτῳ)." The same author read "encampment" (ἀπλήκτῳ). Now, πληκεύειν or ἁπληκεύειν means to pitch camp or to stop for a rest. Theophanes in *Heraclius*: "They encamped (πλήκευσαν) near the city of Nineveh." See my notes on Cedrenus and the *Ecclesiastical History* of Anastasius regarding "he encamped" (ἠπλήκευσεν).
Page 68. 'Αποκρησάριος (Apocrisarios). "As also to become the catholic apocrisiarius of the king." Apocrisiarius. See my notes on Cedrenus, Nicetas, and Anastasius.
Page 84. Αρματα (Armata). Arms. Greek author: "They beached the galleys, and likewise the other small and large vessels immediately, and they will unload (ναταξερματώσουν) the covers (καλυπία) and rigging (ἄρμενα) and arms of every kind." See my notes on Cedrenus, Nicetas, Anastasius, and Constantine Manasses.
Page 171. Ἀρχιευνοῦχος (Archieunuchos). Arch-eunuch, the chief of the eunuchs.
Page 132. Ἄσπρον (Aspron). Asprum. A type of silver coin. For it is *aspron*, white. Index of Greco-Barbarian words in the *Iliad*: *leukolenos*, "white-armed" (ἄσπρες ταῖς ἀγκάλαις). I have spoken of this in my notes on Nicetas and Anastasius.
Page 54 and 58. Αὐθέντης (Authentes). Αυθεντία (Authentia). Lord, lordship. From this comes αὐθεντίζειν (authentizein) or αὐθεντεύειν (authentuein). Greco-Barbarian Index. Homeric *Iliad*: "all-ruling" (*pantanactes*), those who define all things, and act as masters (*authentuontes*). Ducas, pages 62, 69, 129. *Authentizein* in Theophanes. Anastasius translates it as "to act presumptuously" in his *Ecclesiastical History*, and thus it seems one should understand *authenticos* as it is used in Codinus in the *Excerpts*: "Moreover, the vulgar crowd saw the Latins entering the Great Palace as masters (*authenticos*), and suspected that a great evil would be suffered at their hands." The same Anastasius translates *authenticos* as "authoritatively." Today [we have] *aphentes* or *aphentia*, lordship, in the author of the Chronicle which is subjoined to Ducas. See my notes on Cedrenus and Nicetas.
Page 100. Βάλκα (Balka). Barca (a boat). Matthew Paris in [the reign of] Henry III: *Locutus fui cum quadam barca* (I spoke with a certain boat). See my notes on Nicetas.
Page 35. Βάνδον (Bandon). A standard. See the Glossary on Cedrenus and Theophylact Simokattes.
Page 98 and 103. Τὸ Βασμουλικόν (To Basmoulikon). See the same note and my notes on Nicetas.
Page 6. Βεζήριδες (Vezirides). Viziers, advisers.
Page 192. Βεστιπρατήριον (Vestipraterion). Forum of the vestments. See at Cedrenus under πρωτοβεστιάριος (protovestiarios), and at Nicetas under βεστιάριον (vestiarium). And at Const. Manass.
Page 155. Βίγλα (Vigla). Watch, guard. The Continuator of Theophanes in *Theophilo*: "And the leader of such a fleet, the droungarios of the vigla, was called in this way, Orophas." Leo and Basil use this in their *Naumachica*. See the Glossary at Cedrenus, Nicetas, and Anastasius.
Page 99. Βικάριος (Vikarios). Vicar. See at Anastasius’ *Historia eccles.* under that word.
Γ
Page 107. Γαλεώτης (Galeotes). Galiote, brigantine. A small galley, a diminutive of γαλέα (galea) or γαλαία (galaia). For it is written in both ways. Leo, *Tact.* chap. XIX, § 74: "And the galleys." But in § 10 of the same chapter, it is written: "galaia." Matthew Paris in *Henry I*: "And with beaked ships, which they call galeias." Italian *galea*, Spanish *galera*, French *galère*.
Page 111. Γάρδία (Gardia). Παραφυλακή, custody, the guard.
Page 36. Γενητζάριδες (Genitzarides). "With him, silver-purchased slaves whom they call genitzarides." Servants bought with money, whom they call janissaries. Who are also *gianitzarides*. Anonymous author: "He has also fifteen thousand janissaries."
Page 76. Γενητζέρι (Genitzeri). Janissary.
Page 201. Γυρεύειν (Gyreuein). To turn, to return. See the Glossary at Nicetas and the Index.
Δ
Page 56. Δέσποινα (Despoina). Emperor's wife or mother, and on page 8, "of the Despoina Anna." Codinus, *De Offic.* chap. V, Reg. ed. num. 27: "to the Despoina his mother (others say *mana*) and to the Despoina his wife." Theophilus, *Institut.* III, tit. 6, at the end. See at Nicetas. Note by the way: *mana* for mother. Graeco-Barbarous author: "The child lost its mother, and the mother the child." See at Cedrenus under that term.
Page 61. Δεφενδεύειν (Defendeuein). To defend. Hence δεφένδευσις (defendeusis), defense. Acropolites page 6. See at Nicetas.
Page 147. Διακαινίσιμος (Diakainisimos). Diacanesimus. See the Notes, and Jacob Gretser in his observations on Codinus, chap. 14.
Page 30. Δούκας (Doukas). Dux (Duke). See at Cedrenus, and at Nicetas under *kentos*.
Page 87. Δρουγγάριος (Droungarios). Fleet commander. See at Cedrenus, and Constant. Manass. To him were subject the counts. See under "Count."
Page 106. Ἐνορδίνως (Enordinōs). In order. Eustathius II, *psi*: "They stood in order." Theophanes in *Justiniano iuniore*: "Having disturbed the models established in order by his own father," or "in order." Thus *enorinoi dicastai* (officious judges) and *enordinon dicasterion* are said, meaning: ordinary judges, and ordinary court, in Theodore Hermopolites, lib. VII, tit. 2, and in Constantinus Harmenop. *Epitom.* lib. I, tit. IV, § 61 and 62. Which are *ordinarioi dicastai* in *Novella* Coll. 5, *embathmoi*, in the *Basilica*, as *ordinarios archon*, in Synesius, epistola CXLIV. Both are derived *apo tou ordinou*, that is, from "order." Horapollo, *Hieroglyph.* chap. XCIV, or XC: "For these keep themselves watching in order throughout the whole night." Constantine in the *Tactics*: "Having orders (ordinous) of sixteen men." And shortly after: "And the orders of the foot soldiers of each separate unit, etc." Leo, *Tactic.* chap. IX, § 55: "either in order or in battle array." Eustathius at *Il. alpha*: "From the Homeric *obelos* (spit), the graphical sign was transferred, the *obelos*, which is a straight line placed before lines, that is, of orders, outside." Anonymous author, *Panar.* against delinquent monks, found among the writings of Basil the Great: "If someone does not bear the blessing unto his own order." And ibid: "If someone removes his brother from the order of service." Hence *ordinaios*. Glossary ms.: *ordinaios*, thereafter, after these. *Ordineuein* or *proordineuein*. Leo, *Tact.* chap. XV, § 51: "And thus it behooves to pre-arrange (proordineuein) the bands." And *ordiniasein*. Alexius Rharturus *Doct. 1 su.* Graeco-Barbarous author: "And the others he ordered (ordiniase)." *Corona pretiosa*: to prepare, *na ordiniase*. Index in *Iliad. Graeco-Barb.*: He prepares, he puts in order.
Page 145. Ενωτικοί (Enōtikoi). Those adhering to the union, and on the same page, "Union liturgy." "Union." Page 149.
Z
Page 188. Ζαροβοτάναι (Zarobotanai). War machine.
Page 37. Ζατρίκιον (Zatrikion). See the Notes, and what is said at Cedrenus under *tabla*.
Page 86. Ζιχίδες (Zichides). Turkish monks.
Θ
Page 86. Θέμα (Thema). Province. See at Cedrenus.
K
Page 50. Καβούρ (Kabour). Infidel.
Page 119. Καδδηνάλιος (Kaddinaxios). Cardinal. Theod. Bals. at *Nomocanon* of Photius, tit. VI, chap. 1: "as of a cardinal." Thus in the ms. codex which Meursius used, and it does not need emendation as he himself thought. See at Nicetas.
Page 25. Κάδης (Kades). Judge. See Meursius.
Page 8. Καθοσίωσις (Kathosiōsis). Instead of *kathosiōseos*, crime of lèse-majesté. I spoke of this at Theophilus, *Institutionis* IV, tit. ult., § 3, and Salmasius at *Hist. A. Scriptores*.
Page 64. Καλόγηρος (Kalogeros). John Moschus, *Limonar.* chap. 45. Today *kalogeros*. See at Nicetas.
Page 197. Καμπάνα (Kampana). Bell. Hence *kampanarion*, for the tower in which the bell is hung. Astrampsychus, *in Pythagoico laseutēriō*, signifies into a road, both long and high, similar to a tower, a bell-column.
Page 33. Κάμπος (Kampos). Campus (field). Theodore Lector, *Eccles. Hist.* lib. I: "In the litanies which took place in the campus," it occurs often in the same author. Alexander the monk, *de Inventione S. Crucis*: "There appeared to him in the campus, while spending time with the soldiers around noon, a pillar-like cross," which exists in the same words in the hitherto unpublished *Chronicle* of Julius Pollux. Theophanes in *Justiniano Iun.*: "The crowd having been scattered like sheep onto the camps." Index *Iliados Graeco-Barbaræ*: "Plains, the camps." Also *pedion* (plain), *kampon*.
Page 181. Καπιτάνεος (Kapitaneos). Dux, captain. Council of Florence, sess. XXV: "For I sent at that time a captain into Venice to prepare galleys." Council of Ephesus, act. III: "The most magnificent captain wrote to the prince and clergy."
Page 118. Κάρβουνον (Karbounon). *Karbon* in the *Basilica*, coal, and *denrdikardones*, Greeks at 1. 5, § 2, D. *ad exhibendum karbōnarios*, coal-man. Helladius at Photius: "that an anthracite in common usage is a carbonarius."
Page 233. Καρίπιδες (Karipides). Newcomers.
Page 169. Καστέλιον (Kastellion). Castle. Anonymous in *Romano imp.*: "In another very fortified castle." From this *kasteloun*. Theophanes in *Phoca*: "Bearing many castles having in their rigging boxes and icons of the Theotokos." See other Glossaries.
Page 40. Καστελλάνια (Kastellania). Region.
Page 40. Κάστρον (Kastron). Town. Anonymous author in the *Life of Basil the emperor*: "He arms himself against the castle of Euripus." Cedrenus about the same, page 580: "He attacks the city of Euripus." Hence *kastro-logia*, the capture of cities. See at Cedrenus, Nicetas, and the Index of the Gloss. of Anastas. *Historike*.
Page 202. Κάτεργα (Katerga). Triremes. Council of Florence, page 567, German edition. Graeco-Barbarous author.
Barb. Νασύρουσιν τὰ κάτεργα εἰς γῆν A. Vide supra, 1η καπιτάνιος, and the Gloss. to Nicetas.
Page 142. Κέλλη. A monk’s cell. Anonymous in *Constantine, son of Leo*: "The king, having barely passed through the cells of the holy fathers, arrived." Or: "He returned to the cells of the holy fathers."
Page 143. Κελλίον. A small cell. St. Athanasius in his *Sermon on the Images of our Lord Jesus Christ*. It is referred to, moreover, in the first Synod of Nicaea, act 4: "A certain Christian received [it] in his own cell from someone." Theodore the Studite in his *Testament, not yet edited in Greek*: "Do not build a youth into your cell [to serve] as a disciple." An anonymous writer in [the life of] *Leo the Emperor*: "And Samonas says: 'If you wish to be satisfied, send whom you wish to my cell.'" Add Novella CXLI, cap. 1, and Glycas, page 287, Reg. edition. *Κελλίον* is accepted not only for a monk's cell, but also for a small room or inner chamber, even of princes. Palladas, *Anthology*, book 11, tit. 49, epigram 7: "I gave the cell yesterday to the one who sells ptisan." Hence *κελλιῶται* for monks, and for the ministers of the inner bedchamber in Chalcocondyles, page 247, and Phranzes. See also Nicetas, page 326, concerning these. See there the Gloss. on the word *κελλίον*. You may also see the Gloss. to Cedrenus on the same word. In the ms. codex of Chalcocondyles, however, *κελιώτην* is read.
Page 47. Κλεισούρα. A closing, or mountain pass. Joannes Curopalates, page 844: "Through the pass of Podandus, he arrives in Cilicia." Codinus in *Excerpts*: "He was blinded in the pass of the Makre." See to Cedrenus, Nicetas, and Anastasius.
Page 291. Κλητοί. Those summoned (the *evocati*). In the margin of the Royal codex, *Pregati*.
Page 87. Κόμης. A Count (*comes*). He was subordinate to the Drungarius. He who is vulgarly called *comite* today, but *come* by Provincials, differs much from this Count; for this man was in charge of three or five triremes, and was their leader, as the Emperor Leo writes in *Tact.*, chapter XIX, § 23. But the other is intent on the oars, is in charge of the rowing, and sees to it that the rowers ply the oars as the situation requires, or also that they lower or raise the sails. Finally, at a signal given, he instructs them how to ply the oars, and admonishes those who are sluggish in their rowing. Nor does *πρωτοκόμης* pertain to this, who was in charge of the other counts; for today individual counts are put in charge of individual triremes, and one is not in charge of another. However, the Count has one who fulfills his role if he himself is absent, whom we call *subcomes*, or *soutocome*. See Codinus *de Offic.*, cap. 5, num. 93, and the Glossary to Nicetas.
Page 198. Κουρεύειν. To make an excursion.
Page 106. Κουρσεύειν. To plunder. Hence *κοῦρσον*, for an incursion and booty. The *Continuator of Theophanes* in *Theophilus*: "Having caused the raids of the Saracens to be driven to some other place." Another author in *The Life of Basil the Emperor*: "He sent a raid of chosen soldiers against the place called Zapetra." An anonymous writer in [the life of] *Romanus, son of Constantine*: "To drive raids, and take captive, and plunder the Roman land." See to Cedr. and Nicetas.
Page 19. Κράλης. King of the Hungarians, Bulgarians, and Serbs. Joannes Cantacuzenus, book 3, cap. 89: "He provided the title of *Kral* to his son." See the *Pandecta Turcica* of Joannes Leunclavius, cap. 51.
Page 198. Κύρις. Lord. See the Glossary to Cedr. and Anastasius.
Page 198. Κῦρος. Lord. It exists in Chalcoc., page 264, which is also *κῦρ*, *Chir*, in Leonardus Chiensis. Today they also say *χῦρος*.
Page 21. Λαΐνα. An amphora. Today it is called a pottery vessel (*figulus*), *λαϊνᾶς*.
Page 55. Λεγεών. A legion. See the Glossary to Nicetas and Manasses.
Page 202. Δῶρα. Gifts, or wages. [M] 
Page 59. Μαγίστωρ. Who is also *Maistor*. Master. V. Gl. to Constant. Manasses.
Page 164. Μανουάλια. Candelabra. See Gl. to Nicetas.
Page 54. Μαρκέσιος. Marquis. (Who is *Markeses* in Joannes Cantacuzenus, *Hist.* book IV, cap. 3, and Anna, *Alex.* VII).
Page 169. Μεγαλοὺξ. Grand Duke.
Page 58. Μεγαμαγίστωρ. Grand Master. *Le grand Maître de Rhodes*.
Ibid. Μεσάζων. *Μεσάζειν*, Vizier. A counselor. See the Note to page LXXXVIII. A Graeco-Barbarous author in his historical narrative concerning Belisarius: "The *mesazontes* [counselors] of the Great King," that is, as it appears, the counselors of the prince. It has other meanings, or certainly various functions. Joannes Cantacuzenus, *Hist.* book I, cap. 30: "He was *mesazon* to the Empress." They translate: Prefect of the Empress's court, or curator. Hence *μεσάζειν*, in the same book, cap. 15: "He was entrusted with the office of *mesazon*." He obtained the office of administrator. They think him the same as he whom the J.C. calls "Count of the Private Estates." See the annotations of the very learned Jacobus Pontanus to book I, cap. 2. Here Nicetas of Manuel Comnenus, book I, num. 5, page 31 of the Reg. edition, where it is read, must be corrected: "And a care-taker, a *mesevonta* and a servant of personal decrees." For I think Nicetas wrote: "Care-taker and of...," etc., and that word, *μεσεύοντα*, slipped into the context in place of an interpretation, or was perhaps adjoined in the margin from a Graeco-Barbarous codex of Nicetas. For in a Graeco-Barbarous codex it is read thus: "Managing the personal affairs." Therefore *ὑποθέσεις* are *διατάγματα* (decrees); and so that word must be understood in Codinus *de Officiis*, cap. 1, num. 9. In the Index of Nicetas, *μεσάζων* is expounded as a *φροντιστής* (manager). But in the Glossary it is written thus: "*Μεσεάζων*, a care-taker and servant of personal decrees." See to Codin. *de Officiis*.
Page 142. Μονάζουσαι. *Μονάστριας*. Nuns. See the Glossary to Const. Manasses.
Page 40. Μπαιούλος. Bailiff. V. Meurs. in this word.
N. Page 197. Νάρθηκας. For *νάρθηξ*. See to Cedrenus.
Page 51. Νοτάρας. Notary, whom others call *νοτάριος*. See to Cedr.
Ξ. Page 122. Ξόριδες. It was read thus in the written codex which Meursius used. In the Royal codices it is read thus: "And those who are the wardens of the gates, *vixorides*, so called as the messengers of the King."
Ο. Page 140. Ὀρταγή. A comrade. See the note on page 265.
Page 201. Ὀσπίτια. Houses. An anonymous writer in [the life of] *Romanus the Emperor*: "And having made stables and houses, of the best sort, and beds for all the foreigners entering, and those receiving justice, he prepared [them]." The author of the Index to Homer’s *Iliad*, Graeco-Barb.: "Houses, he prepared a home." See Gl. to Nicetas.
Page 268. Οὔλακις. See the Glossary to Cedr. in *κουρσωρ*.
Page 91, 170, 172. Ὀφφικιάλιος. An official, or magistrate. Theodore Hermopolites, book VI, tit. 2, explaining § 3 D. *de Offic. procons.*: "And he is appointed not only to the Proconsul to do no such thing, but also to every official."
Page 76. Ὀφφίκιον. Office. Joannes Curopalates in *Isaac Comnenus*: "He himself first curtailed the giving of offices." Joannes Cantacuzenus, *Histor.* book IV, cap. 55: "That if any of the archons in Wallachia should wish to obtain offices." See Nicephorus Callistus, tom. II, page 519.
Page 9. Ὀψικιοῦν. To augment someone in office and dignity. 
Page 159. Παραπόρτια. Little doors. See to Cedrenus and Nicetas. 
Page 69. Πάτρων. Patron. Horapollo. Book XLVII, "a man fleeing to his own patron." See the Scholiast of Euripides on verse 420 of *Hecuba* and the Basilic Glosses. 
Page 104. Πιγκέρνης. Cupbearer, server of wine. Manuscript Glossaries: *oinocoo*, *pikerne*. Leo Grammaticus: "And the *Aspathes* Georgius, and the cupbearer, and Thomas, the *primikerios*, having been scourged and shorn." Nicephorus Gregoras and Joannes Cantacuzenus use it. In a certain fragment of the Frankish Annals, Eberhard is given the duty of cupbearer. See under *Expantarios*. 
Page 145. Πνευματικός. Πνευματικεύειν. Confessor. To perform the duty of a confessor. The same, page 147. 
Page 91. Ποδέστα. Ποδεστάτος. Ποδεστατία. Podestà, Podestatus. Podestatia. The first and second denote a person. The third, a dignity. Podestatus, says Codinus, *On Offices*, cap. VII, num. 17, (or praetor) of the Genoese in Galata. See Nicephorus Gregoras, book V, sect. 9. 
Page 181. Πλοκάδια. Ropes. See, however, Meursius. 
Page 27. Πόρτα. Satellites of the Turkish emperors. 
Page 190. 156. Πραΐδα. Πραιδεύειν. Booty. To plunder. An anonymous writer in the *Life of Constantine, son of Leo*: "having taken the booty." Leo Grammaticus: "And plundering the things outside the city." See to Cedrenus. 
Page 56. Πρίγκιψ. Πρίγγιψ. Πριγγίπισσα. Prince. Joannes Curopalates, in his *Historical Breviary*: "being a daughter of a prince." In the Basilic Glosses, however, it is written *prigkips*. Joannes Cantacuzenus, *Hist.* book II, cap. 53: "To Taranto, holding [the position of] princess." It is written thus also in the *Alexiad* of Anna Comnena. 
Page 129. Πρωτοοστιάριος. First ostiarius. An *ostiarios* is a doorkeeper. Concerning his duty, see the Notes to Codinus, cap. I, num. 40. 
Page 122. Πρωτοστράτωρ. Prefect of the armies. See Nicetas on Baldwin of Flanders, page 387, c. of the Royal edition. Codinus, *On Offices*, cap. 5, num. 17, seems to mean that the *protostrator* is not called so because he is the first of the imperial *stratores*—that is, of those who helped the emperor onto his horse, about whom we have already treated in [the sections on] Cedrenus and Nicetas—but because he would precede the whole army. The *protostrator*, therefore, about whom Ducas and Chalcocondyles write, is the first commander of war, or prefect of the army. 
Page 202. Πρωτοσύμβουλος. Protosymbulus. Prince of the council. Theophanes: "The *protosymbulos*, having learned these things." The Continuator of Theophanes on Theophilus: "He wished to pray together with the patrician [who was] the *protosymbulos*." See Cedrenus, page 443.
P
Page 30. Ῥήγας. Or Ῥῆγας. King. Those who [are listed as] *rhyades* in the Greco-Barbarian code of Nicetas, in a purer text are *dynastai*. See the Glossary and Index in the same place. 
Page 122. Ῥηγένη. Queen. 
Page 193. Ῥόγα. Ῥογεύειν. Roga, donative, stipend. An anonymous author in the *Life of the Emperor Basil*: "And greeting them with the granting of annual stipends and other rations and gifts." And in [the life of] Constantine, son of Leo: "Having performed the customary distribution of the *roga* among the troops." But to *rogeuein* is to grant *rogas*, to pay a salary, "to provide the daily provision," which is *do- | uouein* in the Greco-Barbarian codex of Nicetas, or to hire with a salary, as in Ducas, page 12, 101, 121, as in Nicetas Greco-Barbarian: to *rogeuein* the infantry or mercenary army, which is *to levy* in a purer codex. See the Glossary to Cedrenus and Nicetas.
Σ
Page 192. Σάγισμα. A horse's covering. See the Glossary to Nicetas. 
Page 182. Σαρακύριδες. Turkish soldiers. 
Page 164. Σουδάριον. Band and fillet. This word exists in the New Testament. *Life of St. Macarius the Roman*, cap. XX: "Then I saw a delicate small band lying on the ground, delightful to the eyes." The Menaea have *soudarion*. Alcuinus, *On the Divine Offices*: "A sudarium, which it is the custom to carry in the hand for wiping away sweat, which by the common name we call a *fanon*." Concerning the *fanon*, see Vossius, book II *On the Vices of Speech*, cap. 6, and in the Addenda. Concerning the *sudarium*, see Gregory of Nyssa, tom. II, page 837; Chrysostom, *Homily 3 on the Epistle to the Hebrews*; Nicephorus Callistus, tomo I, page 173; Oecumenius in *Acts*, page 145. 
Page 17. Σπαθίζειν. To kill, to slay with the sword. Menaea, month of June, day 18: "The holy Theodulus to be put to the sword, and thus their heads to be cut off." 
Page 202. Σπαθίον. Theophanes on Heraclius: "His sword [was] all-gold." See to the history of Anastasius. 
Page 172. Σπεκουλάτωρ. Satellites, Spiculator. Julius Firmicus Maternus, book VIII, cap. 26: "He will make *spiculatores*, who with naked sword cut off the necks of men." Abdias the Apostle, *History* IV: "He asked the *spiculator* that the facility of water might be granted to him." It is also written thus in the *Acts of the holy martyrs Tharacus, Probus, and Andronicus*; and in the *Passion of Abundius and Abundantius*. The Greeks write *spekoulator*, as in the Gospel of St. Mark, cap. VI, verse 27, who [is] a *spiculator* to the old interpreter. St. Athanasius, *Apology* II: "And a *speculator* was present." Manuscript Glossaries: *speculator*, executioner. In the Greco-Latin glossaries, however, it is written *speculator* and explained as *demios*, as from *deine* and *apokephalizo* [behead], because he performed the office of an executioner. And in Suetonius' *Augustus*, cap. 27, and in law *Divos 6, D.* on condemned property, which is book LX, tit. 52 of the Basilic, *[the executioner]*: "But they themselves are also called satellites, or *doryphoroi* [spearmen], *spiculatores* in the old Onomasticon." And so those who think *spiculatores* are called so from *spicula* [spears]. Those who translate [the Greek as] *dioptas* [observers] or *dioptas and investigators* altogether understand them as *speculatores* [watchers]. Certainly, Cujacius, in the cited tit. 52, translates *apoktemnonta* as *spiculator*, and Bonaventura Vulcanius in the *Notes to the Latino-Greek Glossary* writes that it is clumsily confounded there, *speculator* with *spiculator*. Salmasius, however, in his *Notes on Aelius Spartianus*, says it is certain that those who read *spiculatores* are mistaken. Those who have gone to the grammarians for judgment will define this. 
Page 55. Στέμμα. Imperial crown and diadem. Codinus *On Offices*, cap. 10, num. 3 and cap. 20, num. 4. 
Page 188. Σύρ. Lord.
T
Page 184. Ταξείδιον. Expedition. Glycas, page 307. Constantine in *Tactics*: "For they ought always to prepare the things [necessary] for the need of the expedition." 
Page 192. Ταπηστήρια. Rugs. 
Page 140. Τατάς. Preceptor. See the Notes to Codinus, cap. 2, num. 36. 
Page 37. Τέντα. Tent, Tabernacle. See the Glossary to Nicetas. 
Page 39 and 106. Τεντόω. To pitch camp in a place, to pitch tents. 
Page 84. Τζάγρα, Tzagra. So it is constantly written. *Tzagra*, however, is explained as a javelin, page LXXXIV; it is also an instrument by which a javelin is thrown. Anna Comnena *Alexiad* VIII: "Someone of the Latin troops throws [a bolt] through the *tzagra* against the helmet." Hence *tzagrator*, page C., *tzigrator*; *apothysis*, a discharge of *tzagra*;
page XIV, *tzagrobólos*, page CXI, balistarius, Bullialdo. But whether you translate *tzagra* more correctly as *balista* [catapult], and *tzagratoras* as *balistarii* [catapultiers], makes me doubt, because I read *balistarios* in Justinian, *Nov.* LXXXV, ch. 2 and 3; or *balistophoros hamaxas* [catapult-carrying wagons], that is, those containing the so-called *toxobolistras* [arch-throwers], in Leo, *Tact.* ch. 14, § 83. It is written this way also in Theophanes in two manuscripts, and it was not written otherwise in the codex of Anastasius, who translates it as *arcubolistas*. I think, however, it should be read *toxobolistras*. An anonymous author in the *Life of Emperor Basil* writes: *toxobolistrais*, and by the hand-throwing of stones. I also read *cheirobolistras*, that is, *falaricas* [incendiary javelins], in the Glosses, which were launched by hand, regarding which see Servius on verse 705, *Aeneid* IX, and Vegetius. It seems, therefore, that *tzagra* is an instrument other than the *balista*. Nicetas, page LIII, placed *toxotas* [archers] and *tzagratoras* as different things.
Page 201. *Tzantzein*. To afflict with a plague [or defeat]. See Meursius.
Page 122. *Topotērētēs*. Viceroy and governor. It is also used for a legate. An Anonymous author in *Romano Imp.*: "The *topotērētōn* having returned from Rome." Leo Grammaticus: "He sent Michael, the son of Mōroleōn, who was *topotērētēs*." See the *Gloss.* to Cedrenus and Anastasius.
Page 148. *Phakidion*. A miter and headband. Otherwise, cloth. See *Gl.* to Anastasius.
Page 177. *Phamella*. Family. See *Gloss.* to Cedrenus and Anastasius.
Page 146. *Phelōnēs*. Stola. See the *Notes*. Also a penula. See Theophylact Simocatt. book VII, ch. 6.
Page 202. *Phlamouriarēs*. Flammularius, standard-bearer. They used to say *phlamoulon* or *phlampoulon*. See *Gloss.* to Cedrenus, Nicetas, and Anastasius.
Page 61. *Phourka*. Furca [fork]. See *Gloss.* to Cedrenus, Nicetas, and Anastasius.
Page 202. *Phousaton*. So too today. *Fossatum*, an army. See Glycas, page 274, royal edition. *Gl.* to Cedrenus, Nicetas, Anastasius, and to the *Institutions of Justinian*, title *de Milit. testam.* They also write *phossaton* in Ducas, page 33, and in John Cantacuzenus, *Hist.* book I, ch. 48; and book III, ch. 53.
Page 202. *Phoustis*. Biremis [two-oared vessel]. Today *phousta*.
Page 40. *Phrērios*. Frater, brother. John Cantacuzenus, *Hist.* book II, ch. 12: "And of certain *phreriōn*."
Page 116. *Chartoularios megas*. Grand Chartulary. See the *Glossarium Nomicum*, and the notes there.
Page 119. *Chartophylax megas*. Grand Chartophylax. Concerning the *Chartophylax*, see the Sixth Synod, act 1 and 8, page 92, Roman edition; and act 11, page 172; and the Seventh Synod, page 404, at the end; and Meursius. Concerning the Grand Chartophylax, the Council of Florence says: "The Great Chartophylax, Lord Michael." See the *Notes* on Codinus, ch. 1, no. 9.
Page 150. *Chrysoboullon*. Golden bull. A golden seal. The Menologium of August 31 says: "A yearly income to be given to such a person, confirmed by a *chrysoboullon* decree." John Curopalates in the *Breviarium historicum*: "Neglecting their *chrysoboulla* writings." See the *Notes* on Cedrenus and Nicetas, and Spelman's *Glossarium*.
waters even uncorrupted, 307. The prefect of lights has the care of those who are baptized or illuminated, 5. Varangians, called by the English, 261. Thence in English they pray for the Emperor as he reclines, 57. Most faithful guards serve at the doors of the imperial bedchamber and the dining hall, 37. Also their station and rites on the vigil of the Nativity, 49. They make a racket at the table with axes, and they plunder the ambulatory on the feast of the Blessed Virgin, 68. They are always present to the Emperor as he rides, and they escort him to the Blachernae for the Purification, 80. Sword-bearers escort him to the high stairs, ibid. They accompany him to St. John the Baptist, 81, and to the coronation of the Emperor, 94. Their prefect is the Acolouthus, 40. Bardariotes guard the door of the palace, 37. Known by their Persian origin and their Persian accoutrement, the *angourton*, as well as their red garments, and also scepters, with which they maintain order among the people, and whips with which they strike those who deserve stripes, ibid. In Persian they pray for the Emperor as he reclines, 57. The Bardarium river, 38, 267. He hears the prayers of St. Basil the Great at St. Sophia on the feast of Pentecost, 78. He proceeds to the monastery of the same, 80. The Greeks celebrate the feast of St. Basil on Jan. 1st, the very day of the Circumcision, 341. D Regarding the matter of the blessing or sanctification, many years are acclaimed to the anointed Emperor, 79. A privilege, which the Emperor then confers or confirms, has the force of a golden bull, ibid. The blessing or sanctification is received from the *antidoron* or holy bread and from the holy vestments, 288, 365. "Blessed be our God," a canticle during the washing of the feet, 70. The Emperor prays at the Blachernae on the feast of the Purification, 80. And when the memory of the deposition of the venerable robe of the Blessed Virgin is celebrated, 82, 344. The name Blachernae comes from a certain king reigning there, 272. Leo the Great built a temple there, ibid. Where the venerable robe of the Theotokos is [kept], 344. Another Blachernae temple by the seashore was founded by the Empress Pulcheria. Therein [are] funeral linen bands of the Mother of God. Furthermore, the vestments of the Blessed Virgin, part of her girdle, and the head-covering are kept at the Blachernae, 311. John Cantacuzenus was also crowned there for the third time, 368.
Blatinus, the cap of the first secretary, 22. *Blalta*, in Latin purple, 236. It is taken generically for cloth and a wrapper, 237. *Blattium*, imperial attire, 292. Bonefidius corrected, 133. Concerning *antimensia*, 147. Concerning *synaptai*, 151. Branas, besieging Constantinople, was repulsed by the intercession of the Blessed Virgin Hodegetria, 316. What is a *breve* and *brevia*? The protonotary was in charge of preparing them, 133. Trumpeters play while the Emperor rides, 31. On the vigil of the Nativity, during the hours and the liturgy, they stand between the clergy and the flammula, 49. A privilege of carrying the lamp first was given to the archbishops of Bulgaria and Cyprus, 278. Golden bulls engraved with the scepter of the grand stratopedarch and the protostrator, 19. Bulls of the popes, letters and seals so called; golden, wax, and leaden bulls, 256. The Emperor uses a leaden bull in letters to patriarchs and nobles, 34. Bulls are confirmed by the holy cross, 256. A bull added to letters of contracts, and to "bulla," ibid. The bull has the figure of a heart, hung on children against fascination, ibid. Bulloterium, 256. Another *bulloterium*, like a *rationale*, was given to the chartophylax being ordained, 127. *Butta*, a cape or small jug on the *matzuca* of the protologator, 215. It is called *boution, boutéon, bouttion*. Also a wine vessel or a dorsarium, ibid. Byzantium was once under the bishop of Heraclea, 104. Constantine built Byzantium to enlarge his city and handed Rome over to St. Sylvester, 262.
Cabbadium, a garment which is put on over all others, 23. Nobles wear *cabbadia* as they please, from the grand domestic to the grand drungary of the watches, 22. The *cabbadium* of the Emperor, 50. The Despots [wear one of] red, embroidered with pearls, 14. He receives it when he is created Despot, 100. The *cabbadium* of the grand domestic is of double color with borders, 17. Of John Cantacuzenus and his grandsons the Andronici, 18. Red [that of] the grand duke, ibid. Of the logothete of the general treasury, 20. Of the grand primicerius, 19. Of the protostrator, 18. The Caesar was first after the Emperor, 7, 176. The first sebastocrator Isaac Comnenus was set over him, 7. Later the Caesar is made equal to the panhypersebastos by Alexius Comnenus, 8. The formula for addressing a Caesar, 16. The rites for creating a Caesar and a sebastocrator are similar to those of a Despot, 101. 1214 Headgear of ancient times is unknown, ibid. His attire, 15. The Caesar, Despot, and sebastocrator only perform principal duties, 28. Their *scaranica* on feast days is unknown, 14. They say *polychronion* to the Emperor having dined on the vigil of the Nativity, 58. They are not present at the designation of the patriarch, 105. The dignity of Caesar comes from the inauguration, 175.
Imperial shoes are called *tzangia*, 31. Distinguished by eagles, gems, and pearls, ibid. Their use in processions and supplications, ibid. Red ones are put on the future empress, 108. The Despot [wears] bicolored ones with eagles, as if of mosaic work, 13, 225. Citrine shoes of the panhypersebastos, 18. Green of the protovestiarius, ibid. Sky-blue of the sebastocrator, 15. *Caliga* taken for a military shoe, 225. And for breeches, ibid. The ecclesiastical ministry of the *camisatores* concerning coals and heating the cauldrons, 162. Readers wear *camisiae* on the vigil, 44. These are surplice-like vestments of ecclesiastics, 163. Attired in *camisiae* and *epirrhiptaria*, singers at the imperial table sing on Christmas, 60. Bells of the Greeks, 151. The dignity of the *canacleis* office, 80. Palatine, 12. The *canacleus* was neither chancellor nor chamberlain, but more august than either, 206. His scepter [is] a smoothed wood, 13. He does not appear in the retinue, nor at the Emperor's greeting, 12. The *canacleum* is a most august palace and monastery, 206. They are called chancellors and *velarii*, 134. Heraclius permits twelve chancellors, 112. Rails and curtains secluded the magistrates, 134. *Canistrisius*, so called from a *canistrum* (basket), 153. His office is to serve the patriarch when he changes vestments, 156. *Canistrion*, the place for sacred vestments, ibid. The "Great Canon" or the office of the great canon read at the end of Lent, 320. Its matter, author, and time, ibid. The cause of the nomenclature, 321. Its vigil celebrated in the palace, 72, 320. Departure from monasteries is forbidden to virgins by the Trullan Canon, 124. Canonarchs on the vigil of the Nativity hang the image of the born Christ in the palace, 44. Their habit and ministry, ibid. They remove the dishes from the imperial table, 60. The origin and function of the *canonarchs* are proper to the canon or a certain ode, 137.
Cantacuzenus is made grand domestic, equal to the panhypersebastos, 8. He creates his wife’s brothers sebastocratores and grants them unique ornaments, 101. Certain canticles properly termed by the Greeks and accustomed by the Latins, 138, 155. A canticle on the Nativity, 53, 57. You have it in full, 293. Concerning the Epiphany, 60. It is set forth in its entirety, 298. In the adoration of the holy cross, 65. On Palm Sunday, in the washing of the feet, 70, 326. In Easter and the troparion, 73, 327, 328. Canticles accommodated to the Emperor’s coronation, 90, 93, 93. And likewise for the patriarch, 151. Furthermore, while the Emperor sits on the steps, 97. Singers of gilded birds in a bronze tree, 302. The number of singers prescribed by Heraclius and Justinian, 112. They pray for many years for the Emperor, 48, 52. At the table, 57. They sing with *epirrhiptaria* like the Persian Magi kings, and during the singing the Emperor abstains from eating, 60. The first of the singers receives the dishes to be removed from the grand domestic, ibid.
The head covering in the liturgy is a *cidaris* or miter, representing Christ's crown of thorns or the shroud above the head, 169. Consecration is received with head uncovered, ibid. Also prayers must be said with head bared, because of Christ, who is to be honored as the Head, ibid. Yet while [he is] reading [it is] not fitting to sacrifice with head covered, to the mystery, ibid. The honorary boys and relatives of the Emperor minister with head bared, 59, 63. Likewise with the crowned Emperor, 98. And while the patriarch is renounced, 103. The unction of the Emperor being crowned is viewed with heads bared, 90. *Cappa* and *cappasia*, coverings instead of a cap, 266. With hoods removed, the Genoese and Venetians honor the Emperor, 75, 76. The patriarchal prison was called chapels, 124. Monasteries of virgins have the name of prison or chapel, ibid. Carthage was called the third Justina by Justinian, 375. Restored to the Roman empire by Justinian after the Vandals were expelled, ibid. Casaubon’s grammatical trifles, 303, 304. Conducts of the slanderer Baronius, 345. Camps are under the grand domestic, 83. The domestic of the walls takes care of repairing them, 41. In these the prefect of the *tzacones* takes care of the guards, 41. Other guards are there, 43. A sign for moving is given in the tent of the grand domestic, 83. The prefect of the army precedes them when it is time to depart elsewhere, ibid. Cataphracts and cataphractarii [are known] to the Persians as *clibanarii*, 266. On the day of catechesis the people [were] instructed for Lent, 312. On that day the patriarch distributed incense, ibid. Catechist, an ecclesiastical office, 162.
Catechumenea, the place of the catechumens, 365. The emperor, crowned, occupies it, 96. To the catechumens a signal is given by an orarium to depart from the liturgy, 168. A chain of pearls around the scaranicus of the domesticos, 17. Cella, the imperial bedchamber itself, 34, 265. Cellulani, 377. Wax seals the emperor’s letters to his mother, wife, and son, 34. Candles given to the baptized, 145. To the nobles during the reading of the Holy Gospels on the feast of Lights and the fifth weekday of the weeks, 71. At matins on Easter, 73. And to the Podestà of the Genoese, 75. The poor carried a candle during the washing of the feet, 70.
Chæretismos, a Dominical feast along with others of the same kind, 273. Chaganus the Scythian, besieging the city of Constantinople, is repelled by a great miracle and to his own great loss, 322. Chalcedon is not the word to be translated as Χαλκηδών, but Carthage, 378. The Synod of Chalcedon acclaims the holy empress Pulcheria and Marcian, 279. Charatzion, an annual tribute of two thousand florins to be paid by the patriarch to the Turk, 373.
The office of the Chartophylax: to defend the rights of the Church, to judge the disputes of the clergy, the right hand, mouth, and lips of the patriarch, 126, whose charters he guards, 126. To him is entrusted the archive of the church and its privileges, 127. His right of signing, 128. And as magnificent as that of the Roman librarian, ibid. He preceded the bishops in seating, 127. At his ordination, the seal-box and keys are given, ibid. Formerly wearing a tiara, and carried on the patriarch’s horse on the feast of the holy notaries, ibid. He had twelve notaries as assistants, 128. He investigated the customs and life of those to be ordained, 138. No one was admitted to the sight of the patriarch nor to a benefice except through him, 128. Chartophylaceum, the place where the judge of ecclesiastical cases sits, 127.
Chartularius, to the Latins, is a judge of cases, 159. He is reckoned among the lectors, ibid., 160. By Balsamon, the assistants of the officials of the first pentados are called chartularii, 118. Chartularius Magnus, office, 26. Palace, 10. Habit, 22. Prefect of the imperial equerries, 160, 191. From this he leads the emperor’s horse by the bridle, 29. The military chartularius or theme-chartularius enrolled and reviewed the soldier, 160, 191. There was another, the chartularius of the sacred bedchambers, ibid. And the man-chartularius, ibid. And the tabellarii-chartularii, ibid. Vain titles of the Chiote officials, 113. The first falconer carries a chirotheca in his belt with a border and eagles, 25.
Chirotonia, or designation, is made by double suffrage, 104. Among the Greeks, it is equivocally called consecration, election, and designation, and it even provides heretics an occasion for erring and deceiving, 371. Chirotonia, or ordination, is performed by the invocation of the Holy Spirit and the laying on of hands, 372. Chlamys, or the tamparium of the sebastocrator, 15, 224. Dances were led in the Augusteon in honor of the emperor by the prefects of the regions, 357. The domesticus of the first and second choirs is specific, 6, 152. Chosroes sends Sarbarus, the commander of the war, against the city of Constantinople, 321. He is killed by his son, 322.
The sacred chrism was kept in ampullae, monampula, dibampula, 149, 276. How the vigil of Christ’s birth was celebrated in the palace, 43. His image suspended from a tablet, 44. Honored by an imperial kiss, 44, 46. Concerning the same, they are sung at the table, 57. Idiomelon, 298. His Epiphany, or baptism, is honored by the Greeks with a feast, 303. Called the feast of Lights, and for it candles and torches are lit, 304. The Greeks performed the Epiphany for kings on 25 December, 303. The Egyptians kept the Nativity on 6 January, but certain things, 273. Because of Christ, who is our head, he is to be honored, and one should pray even with bare head, 169. Because of Christ, crowned with thorns and wrapped in a shroud, elsewhere the head is sacrificed while covered, ibid.
Chrysobulla, signed with cinnabar and confirmed with the holy cross, to be sent to kings, 32. Chrysoclavarica vestments adorned with golden nails, 221. Chrysoclava and chrysoclavus, ibid. The feast of St. Chrysostom is celebrated in St. Sophia in the presence of the emperor, 80. His commentaries on the Holy Scriptures were consumed in the fire along with the Thomaite, 555. His nativity, 13 Nov., 341. Cibyres, a fountain having the taste of wine due to the miracle of wine made from water, 306. The scenophylax attends to the sacred treasures, 130. They are noted in the *phrourion*, 153.
An imperial lamp adorned with cinnabar, gold leaf, and holy scarlet crosses, 45. Signed bulls, 256. Circitor, or awakener, an ecclesiastical office, 162. The minister of the "circumstatio" attends with the great hetaireiarches and the primicerius of the palace, the protovestiaries, 34. This takes place during the liturgy and vespers, 35. The apocrisiarius and others propose business, ibid. The order of the courtly *circumstatio*, 37. Daily and festive rites, 45. The *circumstatio* at the Nativity, 59. On the Sunday of the adoration of the Holy Cross, 65. At Easter it is performed by the bishops and the primates of the Church, 77. Other rites of the *circumstatio* at the blessing of the water at the beginning of the month, 78, 79. Ioannes, bishop of Citri, and his responses, 117, 162. He reviews other ecclesiastical offices, ibid.
Clamatores, or *kraktai*, are called the protopsaltai, lectors, who sing to the crowned emperor, 93. They hold staves, ibid. The Admiral commands the whole fleet, 59. Each class of courtiers has a fixed place, 37. The senate acclaims the emperor Claudius, 280. Clavatus pileus of the megas domestikos, 17, 235. Of the megas doux, 18. Officials at the table in the Nativity wear hats, 56. Keys given to the ordaining chartophylax, 127. Golden nails, ornaments of garments, 221. Names marked with golden keys, 222. The number of clergy was limited by Justinian, 112. And by Heraclius, by whom the chartulary officials were also defined by a certain number, 112. Palatine clerics are reviewed, 163 sq. Their vestments and ministries, 44. Clibanum, a breastplate, 266. Shields are held by four nobles next to the emperor, on which is his equestrian image, 99.
Codinus, contemporary with or later than Cantacuzenus, flourished shortly before the conquest of Constantinople; he also wrote selections on the origins of Constantinople, 111, 356. How the relatives of the emperor mourn, 106. The collections of the Latins in the liturgy and litanies correspond to the *synaptai* of the Greeks, 151. Collicion given to the emperor by the Podestà of the Genoese, 56. It is a round and delicate bread, 295. Codinus mentions various types of color, 13, 14, 15, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21, 22, 23, 37, 38, 67, 91, 99, 101, 106, 373.
Comes stabuli, or megas kontostaulos, office, 11. Palace, 9. Insignia, 19. Office and privileges, 29. He is the head of the Frankish mercenaries, 33. The count of the imperial horses leads the emperor’s horse, 29. He has the power of riding within the palace, taming horses, training [them], 50. The count (whoever he may be) is the prefect of the curtain-guard soldiers, 38. There are also other protocomites and counts under the megas doux, 28. Commentaries of the new dignities, 7, 174. The *hypomnematographos* is occupied with writing commentaries, 156. (Under) the communion, boiling water is poured into the chalice, 162. Concelli, 119. Consorts of the same cell, 377. Concerning the writing of the name and their number, ibid. The preacher seems to correspond to the rhetor, 114. There was applause for them, 281.
The tomb of Constantine in the church of the Holy Apostles, 81. The scepter of the megas doux and protostrator has golden knobs, 18, 19. The scepter of the prefect of the table has black and gold ones, 21. Andronicus the Younger occupies Constantinople, 316. Besieged three times, it is liberated by the aid of the Blessed Virgin, 321. Andreas Cretensis brought to it the hymn of the Great Canon and the Life of St. Mary of Egypt, 326. Constantine the Great, emperor of the Romans, and his successors were honored by both the Macedonians and the Westerners, 55. He handed Rome to Sylvester, he built Byzantium, 175. Constantine and Irene, under Patriarch Tarasius, restore the holy images, 346. And Constantine Copronymus, the enemy of the images, dies shamefully, 346. Constantinople besieged for seven years during the reign of Constantine Pogonatus, it is liberated by the aid of the Blessed Virgin, 323. Constantine Porphyrogenitus, honored with a new dignity by his father Michael Palaeologus, 174. Constantine Ducas and Constantine Monomachus, about whose times the *roga* ceased, 331, 332. Constantine, under whom the city and empire ceased to stand, 510.
Kontakion, or the book of ordination, is in the power of the hieromnemon, 140. It signifies a book and an *odarium*; it is incorrectly translated generally as a canticle; it is a peculiar kind of canticle, the origin of the word is Greek, it signifies something brief, from *kontion*, 159, 181. Kontakion of the Nativity, 37, 60. Of Palms, 68.
Author of the kontakia, 139. Prefect of the kontakia, 6, 140, 183. Banquets instituted at the coronation of the emperor, the ministers of those dining, the insignia, the feasts, 97, 98. The summoner of the people giving various signals, 151. *Corbant* seems to come from *coryphantius*, 241. The emperor’s crown is kept in the tabernacle to be placed [upon him], not on the holy table, 90. Deacons carry it to the ambo, ibid. The Patriarch and the emperor’s father place it upon him as he is being crowned, ibid. The emperor places a crown of a different form, a blessed diadem, upon the empress who is to be crowned, 92. The emperor lays aside the crown when he is about to communicate, 96. He resumes it after communicating, 96. A crown in the form of a lamp, set round the imperial ornaments, that is, golden and silver wreaths, 218. The crown was first placed on the ground before the Mother of God, 368. *Corte*, that is, *curte*, 266. *Cortelini*, simple janitors of the praetorium, 267. The station, habit, prefect, and *comes* of the *cortinarii*, 37, 28. *Cortis*, the emperor's tabernacle, 38. They raise the radiant (holy) Cross, the customary imperial *flammulum*, 28, 249, 285. They ornament it in the vigil of the Nativity with the third *flammulum* and images of the four holy martyrs, 48, 283. The lamp is adorned with cinnabar and gold leaf, on the feast of the fair held at Jerusalem, 535. The holy Cross, with an image of Christ not made by hands, and the garments of the holy Mother of God, are brought to the host of those besieging Constantinople, 322. The same seals the bulls, called *staurotion*, 167, 141. With candles and incense, it is distributed by the patriarch in the place of the *roga*, 312. Provident care of drinking, 58. Curator of orphans, office, 36. Palace, 11. Habit, 26. The function ceased, 41. There was another ecclesiastical orphanotrophos, 162. Curopalata, office, 15. Palace, 9. His ornaments, 20. He discharges no duty, 54. He was, as is gathered, a prefect of architecture, 261. Called from the care of the palace, described at length by Corippus, 181. The Latins corrupt the word, ibid. The office of *curopalata* or *curopalaticion* granted even to those absent, ibid. Curopalatissa, ibid. The image of the Blessed Virgin *Nicopoia* carried in the triumphal chariot, 315. *Cursarii* called robbers, 255. *Cursores*, who first fought or were placed in ambushes, 255. *Curtobraccia*, short sleeves, 318. Guards of camps and cities, 43. Amictus, 28. Office, 79. Palace, 12. Guardian of the sacred vessels, 159. The privilege of carrying a lamp before him given to the Archbishop of Cyprus, 278. Cyrus, King of the Medes, Persians, and Assyrians, lived according to the custom of various nations, 53. For the sake of ancestral honor, he called himself King of the Persians and received from the Medes the *pilaficia*, *placeolium*, and *epiloricum*, and from the Assyrians the *scaranicum*, *cabbadium*, and the draconteum *flammulum*, 54. His successors were called Kings of the Persians, 54.
D
Damascene plum color refers to the *scaranicum* of the great primicerius, 19. *Decaenneorum*, that is, of nineteen couches, the triclinium and palace, 301. By imperial decree, others are also honored, judging with the first secretary, 36. From the decree in the name of Urban, the defender is understood to be the advocate judge, 131. The first defender or *protecdicus*, serving in an ecclesiastical office, takes care of captives and judges controversies, 130, 131. He is called first in respect to the other defenders, 131. Defenders of the clergy, and indeed of the *exocatacoeli*, were the first, ibid. The dignity of the defender in the Roman Church, ibid. The primicerius of defenders among the regional defenders, ibid. Citrius places four defenders among the ecclesiastical offices, 162. He permits ten Heraclii, 112. The defender of those *proeionymonton*, that is, the plunderers or those fighting before others, 52, 235. The pious custom of the Germans to mourn the deceased, 344. On the birthday of the Mother of God, a procession is held to the *Libis*, 80. Upon her presentation to the monastery of the same, the *Peribleptos*, ibid. At the purification of that [temple], a procession of the emperor’s Varangians visits the Blachernae, ibid. Moreover on the day on which the deposition of her venerable garments is celebrated, 82, 314. Where her burial shroud and the holy veil of her head are kept, 341. The Greeks precede her falling asleep with a fast, 296. Academic fellows perform the service for her assumption with litanies, 314. After her death, she appears alive to the holy Apostles, 299. Called *Panagia* by them, ibid. For other reasons she is also called the Mother of God *Peribleptos*, that is, observable or admirable, 341. Her image, sewn onto the weapons by a soldier, grants a happy battle, 315. The image of the Mother of God *Hodegetria* depicted by St. Luke, the virgin and empress St. Pulcheria builds a temple for, 316. And on the last days of Lent, carried into the palace, it proceeds to meet the emperor and accompanies him as he leaves, 72, 324. The same image of the Mother of God *Hodegetria* and her holy veil, brought to the host of those besieging Constantinople, saved the citizens, 322. The patriarch carried her same image in the *flammeum*, 568. The senate prays for punishments for informers, 280. St. Demetrius represented with a *flammulum*, 48, 283 sq. The deputies, clad in *mandyes*, carry lamps before them when the Gospel is brought to the ambo and the holy gifts to the holy table, 160. The ministries of the deputies are not offices at the palace, ibid. The crowned emperor takes their place, 364. There are also military deputies, 161. Deputatus, 6, 160. The Despot was next after the emperor, 174. His insignia, 13. His habit as a foot soldier and cavalryman, 14. While still a youth, he is bareheaded in the palace, ibid. On feast days, he has another attire, ibid. The formula for addressing a Despot, 16. His ministries on the vigil of the Nativity, 48, 49. The names of the Despots and the emperor are offered up with good prayers, 53 not. In the palm procession, they follow the emperor and the emperor’s sons, 68. Their *flammula* stand behind the imperial [ones], 48. The Despot, Caesar, and Sebastocrator are the only ones said to be *axiomati*, 350. Hence they only fulfill the principal duties, 28. They are not present at the designation of a patriarch, 103. Despot from his own inauguration, 174. The rites of inaugurating them, 99. The Despot is dressed by nobles within the triclinium, 100. He kisses the emperor’s foot, ibid. He receives from the emperor a *stemmatogyrium* or crown, ibid. And the Despots themselves also hold the shield on which the emperor to be created sits, 88. And only the Despots accompany crowned emperors on horseback into the palace, while others go on foot, 97 not. They minister in the absence of the Domestic, 97. They recite the *Polychronion* to the emperor at dinner on the vigil of the Nativity, 58. From this dignity of theirs, the princes of Serbia, Aetolia, and Epirus were called Despots, 173. *Diaconicum*, the sacristy for sacred vestments, 335. The second deacon, the *secundarius*, also from the common deacons, is the first among the common deacons, 150. He is chosen by suffrage, ibid. He is the director of the deacons, ibid. His other four functions; he fulfills the place of the archdeacon and incenses the emperor, 94, 151. He carries the censer and the *omophorion* to the patriarch, 94. The deacons hold the crown to be placed upon the new emperor, 90. They carry it to the tabernacle and the ambo, ibid. They follow the newly crowned emperor, 94. They call the emperor to the holy communion, 96. When he is about to communicate, he hands over the crown, 96. Their number prescribed by Justinian and Heraclius, 112. They wear the *orarium*, 167. By it they give the signal for the catechumens to leave, 168. *Diadema*, whence it was said and made, 50. Formerly it was a military belt, and the holy martyrs were the first of all to be stripped of it, ibid., 289. *Dihampulon* is distinguished from *monampulon*, 149. It is a double ampulla for holy oil and holy chrism, with which the emperor was anointed, and for the sake of the memory of the thing, it was carried, 275, 276. Didymotichus is the first where Cantacuzenus was created emperor, 368. *Divelium*, or the double veil, carried before the emperor on the vigil of the Nativity, 48. It seems the *vexillum* was in place of the *labarum*, a veil consisting of two folds, carried by the *scuterius* or shield-bearer, 269. On the Nativity, 48. The Varangians were present at the same, 39. Doctor of the Apostle, the Gospel, and the Psalter, 143. The domestic, by the emperor’s command, executes the duties, 33. A distinct office, 258. *Domesticus* signifies a leader, a beginner, a prefect, and the first in his own class, 153, 176. The Grand Domestic goes before the *panhypersebastos*, 8. His various offices and ministries at the emperor’s table, in particular, 55. And in the moat, 85. The insignia and vestments of the Domestics, 17. The Domestic of the singers rules the singing, 182 sq., 176. The Domestic of the right and left choir is proper, 6. He is the exarch and serves both clergy, ibid. The Domestic and the protopsaltes of the clergy wear a white shirt, 44. And the Domestic of the psalmists, 152. The Domestic exarch of the Augusta’s clergy is at the same time the Domestic of the church, 163. One Domestic is from among the psalmists, 161. The patriarchal Domestics are placed by Citrius among the ecclesiastical officials, 163. Domestic of the table, office, 20. Palace, 9. His vestments, 21. Ministry, 35. He serves at the emperor’s table, 57, 59, 60, 62, 63. The Domestic, curator of domestic affairs, likewise serves at the emperor’s table, 59. He brings dishes, ibid. He places them on the ground, 60, 62. He brings bread into the *panagiarium*, 62. Domestic of the walls, office, 59. Palace, 11. His insignia, 26. He takes care that the camps are repaired, 41. Domestic of the doors, 6. He is the janitor, 159.
Domestic of the schools, office, 31. Palace, 10. His ornaments, 23. The ministry was once held, now it is null, 36, 261. The Domestic of the themes has insignia common to others; of the Western [themes], office, 72. Palace, 12. Insignia, 27. Of the Eastern themes, office, 71. Palace, 12. Insignia, 27. Both took care of public affairs, 43. Dominica in albis is called variously by the Greeks, 531. Dominica orthodoxiæ was the first Sunday of Lent, so named from the restoration of the holy icons, 345. On the festival of Orthodoxy, a procession was held with the holy cross and holy icons; the decree of Nicaea II concerning the holy icons was read, 346. Dominica festivals are not Sundays, but certain days dedicated properly to Christ, 375.
Sacred gifts are carried with lamps to the holy table, 160.
Donatists do not celebrate the Epiphany, 307.
Dormition of the Blessed Virgin, 343.
The imperial *draconteum*, a small banner carried in the vigil of the Nativity, 38. So named from the dragon, 285. Originated among the Assyrians and adopted by Cyrus, 54. It is an imperial military sign and is accustomed to be carried before the princes of the palace, 283.
Dragoman: by his office he interprets foreign languages, 40. Named from the Chaldean *targum*, 270.
Drunga, 43. Both *drungus* for Greeks and Latins is from antiquity a troupe of soldiers, 189.
Drungary, office, 76. Palace, 12. His insignia, 27. By office he presides over a certain troop of soldiers which is called a *drunga*, 43. The drungaries are subordinate to the Grand Duke, 28.
Grand Drungary of the fleet, office, 32. Palace, 10. His equipment, 23. He is likewise subordinate to the Grand Duke, 56.
Grand Drungary of the vigils or watch, office, 84, 189. An uncertain number of soldiers is subject to him, ibid.
Grand Duke, office, 7. Palace, 9. His ornaments, 18. He is the chief at sea, 28. He raises the imperial banner, distinguished by an equestrian statue, ibid. Subordinate to him are the Grand Drungary of the fleet, the Admiral, Protocomites, Counts, and Drungaries, ibid. Hence *doukas*, *doukas*, *dous*, *doukator*, *doukades*, *doukai*, *doukaina*, the prefecture and the coinage, also *megaducas*, 178.
[E] Lest they approach the imperial horse recklessly, it is not bugled after dinner, 32.
The offices and officials of the Great Church of Constantinople are numbered by Codinus in the preceding index and in chapter 1 and following. They are explained in Book I, comprising sixteen chapters. Citrius puts three prefects of the churches or ecclesiarchs, 158. The church leaders defer the names of those three designated for the patriarchate to the emperor, 102. The emperor promises to create a son and defender of the holy church, 87. In all churches, water was blessed on the first day of the month, 506. The Church is decorated with its own ornaments, i.e., holy icons, 316.
The election of the patriarch and of the archbishop of Ochrida was made by one scrutiny, 375. In it, there was acclamation, 279.
Emmanuel, the penultimate emperor, renovated the porphyry column, 340.
The sword signifies power, 51. It is carried before the emperor on Easter, 74. The imperial sword is kept in the *rucharium*, 31.
Enthronement is in the power of the *hieromnemon*, 138. Enthronement and the right of *contactus* are distinct offices, ibid.
Eparch, office, 23. Palace, 10. His attire, 21, 14. Administration: none, 35.
The *ephebi* serve the crowned emperor, 94. They are bareheaded and kinsmen to the emperor, 59, 63, 105. One carries the emperor’s sword in the vigil of the Nativity, 52.
*Epicombia*, gifts for the creating of an emperor, are sent into the populace, 88. And to the emperor when proceeding to other palaces, 98. What *epicombia* are, how much money is tied to them, and when they are thrown, 88, 355, 356. Their further distribution to courtiers and soldiers, ibid.
*Epigonation*, a sacred vestment of bishops, 140. It signifies the bonds and the linen cloth of Christ, ibid. The *epigonation* hanging from the loins over the knees denotes the linen of Christ washing the feet and the *rhomphaia*-like strength of the loins and victory, 188.
*Epiloricum*, sprung from the Medes, used by Cyrus, 51. It is an upper garment or super-loric, 239. The *epiloricum* of the emperor, 50. Likewise, as it pleases, the nobles from the Grand Domestic down to the Grand Drungary of the Vigils, 22. The *logotheta* of the course carries it, 22. First Secretary, 22. *Logotheta* of domestic affairs, 24. Grand *logariastes*, 21. Prefect of petitions, 24. *Logotheta* of the army and *logotheta* of the revenue, 21. Four nobles have golden *epilorica* with shields, just like the standard-bearer, 99.
1220 *Epimanikion*, a sacred vestment of bishops, 140, 169. Its meaning and the prayers of the one putting it on, ibid.
Epiphany, so called because of the three appearances of Christ, 303. It was also called the Theophany and the festival of lights, 303, 305. The Latins celebrate one, the Greeks another, 503. The Donatists celebrate none, nor the blessing of the waters, 65. Because of the Epiphany of Christ appearing in such a miracle at the wedding when water was turned into wine, 306.
Clothing themselves in *epirrhoiptaria*, the chanters sing at the imperial table during the Nativity, 60.
Bishops: their day, 5. On the Paschal day in the palace, they visit the emperor with the patriarch, 77. They perform the circumsession, ibid. They kiss the cheek and hand of the emperor, ibid. Twelve are gathered for the election of a patriarch, 102. They are chosen by double suffrage, major and minor, 101, 374. They stir up a dispute with the chartophylax concerning seating, 120, 127. Only bishops are permitted to wear the *epigonation*, 140. They sacrifice bareheaded, distribute the gifts, and pray well for the people, 288, 363. Dressed in undulated palls, they venerate the emperor, 105, 380. Their undulated *sticharion* and *gammadium*, 166. The bishop of Heraclea consecrates the patriarch in Saint Sophia, 104.
Banquets instituted for the coronation and weddings of the emperor, 97, 98. Their rite, ibid.
Knight-Count, 29, 5, 19.
Knight-Despot, in what habit, 14.
Knight-Emperor, and on foot, 29, and about to receive his bride, 107.
Equestrian image of the emperor on the shields of 4 nobles, 99. On the *scaranikon* of the Grand Drungary of the Vigils, 21. On the banner which the Grand Duke raises, 28. Also on the banner which is carried in the vigil of the Nativity, 48, 283, 284. Equestrian apparatus of a despot, of a sebastocrator, 15, 229.
Imperial horses’ ornaments in the coronation, 97. This custom has largely ceased, ibid. The patriarchal horse, on which he is carried to be consecrated at Saint Sophia, has a white covering, 104. Also a patriarchal horse, given upon creation by the Turkish sultan, 252. Empty or saddled horses prepared for the emperor, i.e., called *alogia*, 250. Empty horses of the soldiers are also called *ouptol*, ibid. The bride enters the palace on a horse, 108. Hunters of leopards, and those who carry ice to cool drinks, 108. On a versatile horse, apples are carried to the table of the emperor and he is himself raised near the canopy of the emperor, 301.
Four Gospels are read on the first days of Great Week, 70. And how this four-book codex of the Gospels is read, 317. Twelve Gospels are read during the vigil of Preparation, the emperor holds a candle, 71, 319. Once in a year the whole Gospels are read, 319. The codex of the Gospels bears the figure of Christ, 314. It is carried to the ambo, preceded by deputies with *mandya* and candles, 160.
Two eunuchs support the empress to be crowned, 91. Supported by the same, Luitprand is conducted to the emperor, 302.
Exarch is a legate or vicar of the patriarch, also a collector of the Turkish subsidy, 157. He used to excommunicate the guilty and absolve penitents, ibid. The Exarch was also an imperial or palatine dignity, whence *exarchate*, *exarchia*, 158. Exarch is the *protopsaltes* of the emperor, 94. The office of the *excitator*, 162.
Excubitor or president of the sacred bedchamber, office, 60. Palace, 11.
Those who make excursions and carry out plundering are defended by the Protostrator, 32. Nor do they have a banner or order, ibid.
Generals of armies used to wear torques as insignia, 51.
Exocatacoeli are approached through the *protonotary*, they have their own churches, 66, 119. The patriarch gives them the power of wearing the *phelonion* when ordained, 66. From these the Archdeacon is designated by the emperor, ibid. How the word is to be written against Junius, 118. What the notion of the name is, ibid. For these, some conjecture, 119. Patriarchal *exocatacoeli* were deacons, formerly also priests, 120. Great authority, they sit in the synod with the patriarch, ibid. A dispute was stirred up by them, because they were prior to bishops in seating; a rescript was issued for them, ibid. They were like cardinals in the Greek Church, 128.
Execrations and synodical imprecations against certain heretics, 281. The Senate against the Emperor Commodus, 280.
F
Imperial servants carry away the dishes, 58, 5. The scattering of *epicombia* is made upon them, 355.
Sepulchral bands of the B. Virgin, the head-covering in the Blachernae, founded by S. Pulcheria, 311, 345. Band of Christ [original-script column: see facsimile]
deposed in the Hodegon temple, ibid. A feast is also dedicated to it, ibid. Emperors Justinian and Heraclius decree that there should be forty deaconesses, 112. Women used to wear small gospels around their necks, 319. The *magnus sacellarius* oversees convents for women, 122. Those locked in cells or prisons are forbidden to go out, 124.
