How imperial dishes are brought in, set down, and when placed on the ground are stored in the *imensa*, 62. From how many and what kind of services they are removed from the table, 58, 60. The *domesticus* of the table removes them from the table and gives those that are to be removed to the prominent men, 57. Those who carry the *scaranica* carry them to the doors for purple, 58. The *magnus domesticus* gives to the cantor, the *domesticus*, the lampadary, and the master, 60. The same man summons readers and the emperor’s nephew, the *panhypersebastos*, to that service, 60, 61. In the same place are occupied the *protovestiarius*, nephews, honorary boys, and *primiceri* along with the Varangians, courtiers, and stationaries, 61. Finally, the emperor himself gives a dish to the *magnus domesticus*, 62. The *domesticus* retains the dish’s vessel, ibid. 17. The third feria after the Sunday of Shrovetide is the feast of the *catechesis*, 312. The fifth feria of Holy Week is itself also called "Great," 70. On it, the washing of the feet of the poor is performed, 70, 71. On the same day, the vigil of the preparation happens, and the twelve gospels are read while the emperor holds a lamp and the nobles hold candles, 71, 319. During the remaining days of Holy Week, the four holy gospels were read and divided, 70, 333 (chalis). On the fifth feria, that is, the feast of the Paschal or *diakainisimon*, the patriarch, bishops, and archimandrites approach the emperor, form a standing circle, offer incense, and venerate him with a kiss, 77. Feasts celebrated in the palace, 43, 79. Feasts of ten days or more are celebrated with splendid robes and banquets in honor of the crowned emperors, 98. Five solemn feasts, on which the emperor takes food in the *triclinium* with service from the nobles, 64. Four principal feasts, before which the Greeks observe Lenten periods, 296. The feast of the Nativity in Constantinople was performed before the times of St. John Chrysostom, 301. The feast of All Saints was performed on the first Sunday after Pentecost, 297. The feast of the SS. Notaries turned into a revelry (*bacchanalia*); the abuse was corrected, 271. On the feast of the Annunciation, Palm Sunday, and Holy Saturday, purple *sticharia* are worn, 67. The unique rite of feasts celebrated in monasteries, of preferring a veil and lamp, which when the emperor arrives is lit and placed over the veil, 82; the causes, rites, and processions for other feasts are held in their own places, 43, up to 82, 111. Commentary, ten chapters. The sceptre of the *magnus domesticus* consists of fibrous marks, 17. The *scaranicum* of the *magnus dux* has an image of the emperor seemingly carved after the manner of fibers, 18. Of the *protostrator*, ibid. Of the *magnus logothete*, 19. Of the *magnus stratopedarch*, ibid. The sceptre of the *magnus myrtaite* represents the figure of the letter gamma, 27. Likewise *sticharia*, 166. Other vestments, 221. The emperor’s son, when crowned, also carries a holy cross, an *acakia*, and a wreath, 52. He is visible from his knees in the oratory, ibid. He follows his father in processions, 68. He keeps watch in the camp, 85. Together with his father, he also renounces the patriarch, 103. In his father’s coronation, by his permission, he places his own name before the profession of faith, 86. A fringe made of *margella* used to cover the ear of the Medean judges. Fringed caps of the emperor’s sons-in-law, 15. Of the *sebastocrator*, ibid. Of the *caesar*, ibid. Of the *magnus primicerius*, 19. A fringed *margellia* has the cap of the first secretary, 22. *Cabbadium* of the *magnus domesticus*, 17. Fringed *scaranicum* of the same, ibid. Of the *magnus dux*, 18. Of the *logothete* of the general treasury, 20. Those who wear fringed gold and scarlet caps at the emperor’s table on the Nativity pray well, 55. The patriarch’s *flammeum* or head-covering was white, afterwards illustrated with gold and adorned with images, 368. Called *flammularis* is he who carried the *flammulum*, 283. The emperor has one or two *flammula* in an expedition or army, 48. The *magnus primicerius* has his own in the imperial troop, 35. But those who go on excursions do not have their own, 52, 12. The *domesticus* gives each one a place in the great trench, 84. But those of the nobles are not unfolded before the emperor’s, 83. *Flammulum* is said and written in various ways, 282, 283. It is not the same as a *flammeolum* or a *labarum*, ibid. And they are distinguished by twelve total signs and images. Behind the imperial ones in the vigil of the Nativity stood the *flammula* of the nobles, ibid. 16. The accustomed *flammulum* of the emperor was a holy radiant cross, 28, 249. Or *theostaurion*, 283. Another was the *drakonteion*, ibid. *flammulum* having the images of the *theodoron* is of both St. Theodores, 286. *Flammula* taken to the vestiary, 53. Omitted in mourning, 69. The arcade is strewn with flowers on the Saturday of Lazarus, 67. The bishop’s *sticharion* has rivers, 166. They signify the grace of teaching and the rivers of water flowing from the Savior’s belly, ibid. Cups float upon them, ibid. Also having robes as rivers, or undulated garments, ibid. The font of healings is the temple outside the city called *Pegē*, 381. Brides were received there, 382. The form of imperial crowds is diverse, 31. Formulas for subscribing to a conferred benefit, 79. And how Sultan Mahomet created Gennadius patriarch, and how the Christian emperors created patriarchs, 374. The *forum cupedinarium* is called post-*Augusteon*, 353. The trench (*fossatum*), named from a ditch, signifies camps fortified by trenches, ibid. In the *fossatum*, the head of the imperial troop is the *magnus primicerius*, 33. The head of the entire *fossatum* is the *magnus domesticus*, 28, 83. In it, the emperor holds the place of the absent one, ibid. He arranges the stations; he dies with the *adnumiasta*, 85. How diurnal and nocturnal vigils are constituted in the *fossatum*, 84. *Fossatum* light, i.e., soldiers of light armor, ibid. The Franks are called Genoese and Venetians, 76. Or even Italians and Sicilians, 330. The head of the Frankish stipendaries is the *magnus conostaulus*, 33. *Francopulus*, *Turcopulus*, *archontopulus*, etc. This distinction sometimes means nothing, and sometimes diminishes, 228. Part of the bridle is golden, *orea*, said from *aure* (ear) and *ore* (mouth), 227. In the manner of rope-work, the knot in the sceptre of the *magnus domesticus* is wrapped in twisted silver, 17. Of the *magnus dux*, 18. Varied functions of clerics, 113, 273. Of courtiers, soldiers, servants, 28. *Fundas accubitor*, slightly square, 185. Cords made of gilded leathers, with which the fruits of the emperor’s table are set, 301. Dusky color, a medium between white and black, 19.
G
Galata or Pera or Sycena is the thirteenth region of the city, 122. The *potestatus* of the Genoese inhabits it, 56. The kings of France used to lead the banner of St. Dionysius, called the *oriflamme*, into expeditions, 286. *Gammadia* *sticharia*, 166, *gammatæ* vestments, 221. *Stemmatogyrium* of the despot’s son-in-law, 100. Caps, 14. Among them is the order of dignity, 6. Mahomet II creates Gennadius Scholarius patriarch, 372. He yields the office, being variously pressed, 373. Knees are not bent in the ecclesiastical rite between Pascha and Pentecost, 331. The Genoese *potestatus* bends his knees twice to the emperor and kisses his hand and foot, 75. The Genoese prominent men have the same honor toward the emperor, ibid. With hats removed, they bend their knees and shout auspiciously, ibid. Their *praetor* is present at divine service on the Nativity of Christ, 56. He receives the *collicium* from the emperor, ibid. He holds a candle with his own during the Paschal matins, 74. The Greeks celebrate the feast of St. George the martyr on April 23. A procession on his feast day, 81. He is called "Great" by the Greeks, ibid. His image, 69, 98. Standing next to it, the crowned emperor throws coins to the nobles, ibid., 6, 7. This image of St. George bleeds, and the horse terrifies the elder Andronicus with a double and ominous neighing, 313. Gerasa, a fountain, becomes wine because of the miracle of wine made from water, 306. Gerlachius, a Lutheran preacher, kisses the robe of the Turkish emperor in a memorable act, 328. Germans, the thirtieth of the Assumption of the B. Virgin, which they celebrate, 343. Steps, high outside the Blachernae temple, 325. The emperor is led to them, 80. The emperor follows the *Hodegetria* to the same, 72, 325. Recent Greeks vary foreign expressions, 112. They boast with the clatter of empty titles, ibid. They delight in the pretentious title of *megalou*, 120. *Ei tou protou*, 577. They are monsters of titles, *protonobelissimos* and *protonobelissimoupertatos*, ibid. They decorated the emperor’s lamp with a double crown because of his care for divine and human ornaments, 278. They ought to have seen that power exercised in appointing and deposing patriarchs, 372. And hence the Greeks taught the Turks even to treat patriarchs by their own example, ibid. Greeks before certain principal feasts observe a fast or Lent, 59, 296. They celebrate from the feast of All Apostles, 297. They celebrate the Assumption of the B. Virgin with soul and body on the fifteenth of August, 15. Nones, odes, *stichi*, *idiomelon*, 138. The diversity of Greek and Latin rites does not stand in the way of union, 296. Their error, that a priest cannot serve in the place of a deacon, refuted by Calecas, 137. They celebrate the liturgy in Lent and fasts after midday, 282.
Granatzam, an Assyrian garment; the Emperor wears it with flying sleeves; the Grand Domestic wears one sleeve, others wear the *lapatza* pulled in on both sides, 63, 301. They have a rim to the caps of imperial kinsmen, 13, 231. And a rim or border of a *trulla* on the cap of the First Secretary, 22, 242.
H
Heresy. The effect of the same, 372. Heretics change the names of ecclesiastical offices, to signify the dignity of the order, ibid. The bones of the Manichaean, Nestorian, and Eutychian heretics are to be exhumed and synodical imprecations [uttered] against them, 281. The great fast of the Tetradite heretics, 334. 
A week is also called "the week of weeks," 317. On the fifth day of the fifth week of Lent, the Great Canon is chanted, 320. And on Saturday, the Akathist hymn to the Blessed Virgin, 321. The office of Holy Week, the rite, the reading of the Gospels, the washing of feet, 70, 317 up to 327. Use of the week of Renewal or *diakainesimos*, with the holidays, is the paschal week itself, 333. It is free from fasting, ibid. 
Helena (St.) and Constantine the Great; the Greeks celebrate their feast on May 21, 342. 
Helena, daughter of Cantacuzenus, married to John Palaeologus, 382. 
Heraclius limits the number of clergy, 112. He wants only two syncelli, 377. He melts down church vessels for money, invades Persia, 327. He leads the image of the Mother of God into battle, 313. 
The Greater Hetaeria, a military cohort whose commander is the Grand Hetaeriarch; and the Lesser, whose commander is simply the Hetaeriarch, 190. 
Hetaeriarch, palace official, 12. Vestments, 26. His function, 42. Hetaeriarchs call others to the circumstation, either from those reconciling friends or from the military cohort, 190. He is also called the Megaleteriarch and the Megaeteriarch, ibid. 
Grand Hetaeriarch, palace official, 10. His insignia when the Emperor is about to go out; he calls the nobles to the circumstation, 34, 22. He proposes business in the circumstation, 25. His service at the imperial table, 56. 
Heterodox [people] grow angry hearing [of] the kissing of the Supreme Pontiff's foot, 328. The same [people], however, patiently, humbly, and with bended knee kiss the Emperor’s robe; and the Turks do the same, ibid. And they sing hymns to their own feet, honored in turn by a kiss, 329. 
The Hierarch holds the Holy Gospel over his head while he is being consecrated, 169. 
The office of the Hieromnemon, 137. He assists the *kanstrision* in vesting the Patriarch; if he is a priest, it is not permitted for him to perform the office of a deacon, due to the error of the Greeks, ibid. His three offices of inspecting prayers or suffrages, the right of enthronement and the *kontakion*, are explained more fully, 138, 139. 
The Megaura palace prepared for the envoys of the Spaniards, 302. 
Image of the Virgin Hodegetria, 70. For the sake of piety, it was brought into the court from the monastery, 315 sq., 324. With what honor it was received and returned by the Emperor, and how long it stayed in the palace, ibid. and sq. 
The Hodegetrion or Hodegium, a temple built by St. Pulcheria, and a monastery; hence the image painted by St. Luke and reposited there found the name "Hodegetria," 316. 
Various rites for honoring the Emperor, 278, 251, 372. 
Honorary youths pray well for the Emperor, being themselves joined, bareheaded, 59. One carries the Emperor’s sword on the vigil of the Nativity, 52. He takes the dish from the hands of the *panhypersebastos*, 61. He holds the footstool, 63. The Emperor’s lamp is handed to one during the interrupted reading of the Gospel on the Thursday of Holy Week, 71. Another, on Palm Sunday, at the end of Matins, gives the signal by plucking a branch from the cloister to strip it, 68. On Great Saturday, they fill the triclinium with laurel, 73. The crowned Emperor scatters coins among them, 98. They serve at the table, 98. 
What the hours are like and how the liturgy is chanted on the vigil of the Nativity, 45. In what habit the Emperor attends, 47. At the beginning of the hours, a circumstation is formed, 45. On Good Friday and Easter, 72. 
When the enemy is near, vigils must be observed twice, 84. 
Hulac, the Turco-Ionian envoy, 245. 
Morning hymn, 37. Evening hymn, 47. The Emperor hears evening hymns in St. Sophia on Easter, 76. 
The Trisagion hymn is chanted at the washing of feet, 70. On Easter, 76. At the coronation of the Emperor, 89. Emperors rise up to it, 93. 
Hyperpyrum, a species of coin, 33, 259. Hyperpyron, a head-covering like a flaming fire, 291. 
I
The destruction of the iconoclasts, 316.
Iconostasion
is not a Calvinist shrine, but a place for placing or setting up images, 24. 
Idiomelon, a proper chant for a feast, 298. 
Images (Holy) of Christ being born are hung with others from a board on the vigil of the Nativity, 14. Priests stood near them, ibid. A synod was collected to restore the holy images, 316. They were called the ornaments of the church and restored to it, ibid. Appropriately honored on the Sunday of Orthodoxy and in the procession, in which the Emperor shared, and the decree for their veneration was read in St. Sophia, ibid. and 82. Holy images were also brought down in the Palm procession, the Emperor and his son, the despots, and the patriarchs leading the way, 68. In the procession to the Porphyretic column, 79. 
Image of the Emperor on garments, 235. And on the left side of the *skaranikon* of the Grand Domestic, 17. Equestrian image of the Emperor on the *skaranikon* of the Grand Drungary of the Watch, 21. On a *flammulon*, 48. On the shields of four nobles, 99. The image of the Emperor sitting on the throne is on the back of the *skaranikon* of the Grand Duke, 18. Of the Protostrator, ibid. Of the Grand Logothete, of the Stratopedarch, 19. Of the Grand Primicerius, ibid. Of the Grand Drungary of the Watch, 21. Image of the Emperor standing and crowned on the further part of the *skaranikon* of the Grand Domestic; the Emperor [is] under the appearance of transparent glass on the *skaranikon* of the Grand Primicerius, 19. Of the Grand Conostaulus and of the General Logothete, ibid; [on] bullae, 256. 
Images of coins, from which one can know what the diadem, the Justinian [diadem], [and] the blattion are, 237, 289, 292, 237. Others with a stole, 167, 308. 
Emperor’s *Alio*, 6. The Caesar was first after the Emperor, 7. The epithet "Sebastos" is proper to the Emperor, 8. But the Sebastocrator was constituted as it were an emperor, 7. The right of innovating things and of putting words before or after belongs to him, 8. The Emperor does not go out to Matins on the vigil of the Nativity, 43. And he is present or absent at the Liturgy, 46. By what rite he ascends into the oratory, 28. His various garments, 47, 50. Habit, ornaments; he orders [Constantine?] to be mindful of it, 31. He always carries the Holy Cross in his right hand, ibid. He wears his daily garment at table; when the Emperor is brought in, he rises, 62. Many years are sung to him as he partakes of the *panagia*, 63. He eats five times in the triclinium, with the nobles serving, 64. Emperor’s habit on Palm Sunday, 15. The procession, ibid., 69. By his power the ambulatory is despoiled, 68. Feasts are celebrated less splendidly in mourning, 69. The Emperor, in a white garment, stands before the victorious Mother of God; he wears a white super-tunic in the procession, 69, 70. On the Thursday of the Lord’s Supper, he pours water into a basin, washes the feet of twelve paupers, wipes them, and kisses them, 70. He holds a lamp while the Gospels are read, 71. He puts it down and receives it, 71. He chooses a room for himself to hear the four offices of the vigil, 72. He meets the Hodegetria and conducts it, 72. At the liturgy of Great Saturday, he carries the permitted vestments, ibid. Laurels are scattered before the Emperor, ibid. During the divine office of Great Pascha, the lamp is given to the Emperor again, which he kisses, 74. Hearing Vespers in St. Sophia, he is honored with the offering of incense, 76. He ceased to enter the tabernacle and to distribute gold, 77. He is visited by the patriarch and bishops, and the service of the circumstation is performed by them for him, ibid. He is honored by the bishops with incense and a kiss, and he honors them in turn, ibid. On Pentecost, he is dressed as he pleases, ibid. He hears the prayers of St. Basil in St. Sophia, 78. The Holy Cross is placed on his forehead, [the item] which confers the same force as the golden bull, ibid. A fifth part of the spoils is given to the Emperor, 85. The Emperor, about to be crowned, occupies the palace, 86. He affixes his name to the profession of faith written by his own hand, and subscribes to it, ibid. He embraces Apostolic traditions, ecclesiastical customs, and the decrees of the councils, 86. Sitting on a shield, he is lifted on high by his father, the patriarch, and the nobles, 88. He is greeted with auspicious acclamations, 89. In St. Sophia, he is vested with the saccos and diadem previously consecrated, 89. He sits sublime on an anabathra, with his mother wearing a crown, and his wife wearing a circlet, 89. The patriarch, having prayed for the Emperor, anoints him in the form of the holy cross, while the patriarch and the people sing and repeat "Holy" and "Worthy," 90. Crowned by the patriarch and his father, ibid. The Emperor holds the Holy Cross, the Empress a palm, and, the Trisagion having been sung, he rises, 92, 93. The Emperor, called by the deacons, proceeds to the prothesis and, having put on the *mandyas*, holds the ecclesiastical place of the deputy, and walks in front of the line of supplicants with a great retinue, 95. Honored with incense and chant, he sits on the anabathra and rises for the reading of the Symbol, for the elevation of the Holy Body, and for the Lord's Prayer, 95. About to communicate, he is called by the deacons and incenses the sacred table in the form of the holy cross, 95. The patriarch returns the honor of incense to the Emperor, 96.
tonia*, and offers an occasion for error, or for heretics to be deceived, by the calling and the laying on of hands, ibid. And the same even with head uncovered, 169. Ordination of the patriarch took place in St. Sophia, 127. Among the priests and deacons, the first doorkeeper guarded the doors, the second the flask of holy chrism, 149. The prefect of the sacred station oversees the order among the sacred rites, 38. The *orea* is called the part of the bridle around the mouth, 227. It is sung with organs at the Nativity of Christ, 52, 53. At the two *orgiai*, the Emperor encounters the protopapas during the benediction, 79. Ornaments of the nobles, 13, 17. Orphanotrophos (ecclesiastical), 162. There is also an office, 56. [of the] palace, 11. Insignia, 26. Formerly he cared for orphans, afterwards it became vacant, 41. On the Sunday of Orthodoxy, the Emperor visits St. Sophia, 82. It is the first Sunday of Lent, not the next after Pentecost, 343. So called on account of the holy images restored by the seventh general council, 88, ibid. With these and with the holy cross a procession took place, and the synodic document or decree concerning the repair of the holy images was read, the Emperor standing, 346.
Orthography of foreign words is varied, 112. On *monampulion*, 148, 275. On *secretarius*, 141, 241. On the *megas doux*, 178. On the *contostaulos*, 181. On *allagion*, 200. On *tamparion*, 224. On *copritorion*, 226. On *tupha*, 227. On *cabbadion*, 229. On *authenta*, 232. Various orthography regarding *chrysoclavi*, 233. *Blattae*, 256. *Epiloricia*, 239. *Phaceolion*, 240. *Amiralios*, 243. *Fossaton*, 214. *Matzuca*, 215. *Botta*, ibid. *Tzangia* and *sagmata*, 252. On *flammulae*, 379. The custom of kissing the eyes, the face, the right hand, and the feet, 328. Heterodox people mock the custom, yet they approve by deed and writing of the kissing of the Turkish robe, and even by admitting the kisses of intimate submission they are satisfied, 529. The patriarch at the Council of Florence kissed the Pope's cheek, 120. He kissed his right hand and cheek, ibid. Justinian decreed that there should be no more than one hundred doorkeepers, as there were in the time of Heraclius, 112. The first doorkeeper guards the doors under the ordination or election of ecclesiastical ministers, 148. The second holds the *monampulion* of holy chrism for the anointing of bishops and priests, 149.
P
*Paegniota* in the vigil of the Nativity under the liturgy and the hours stand between the clergy and the *flammulae*, 49, 288. Monastery of the Palaiologoi built by them, 80. Order of the palace offices, 6, 171. The feast of Palms is called by the Greeks the feast of the *Baia*, 311. On the feast, the patriarch and the emperors used to give *rogai*, ibid. The patriarch gave candles and holy crosses, 312. *Paludamentum* is wrongly called *mandyas*, 379. The *Panagia*, having been raised from the table, is brought in and offered by the *megas domestikos* to the Emperor, 62, 63. Once the *Panagia* is received, a *polychronion* is chanted, 63. The custom of consuming the *Panagia* arose from the apparition of the Blessed Virgin and from the apostles, 299. The blessing was not given to it from the consecration of priests from heaven, ibid. *Panagia* is an epithet given to the Blessed Virgin appearing after her death by the apostles, ibid. Thence the name of *panagia* was transferred to the bread, 300. *Panagiarion*, a dish in which the *panagia* was carried, 298. The dignity of *panhypersebastos* was newly devised by Alexios Komnenos, 7, 177. Equal to the Caesar and the *megas domestikos*, soon inferior even to the *protovestiarios*, 7, 8, 18. His insignia, ibid. The bread placed on the cushion by the apostles in place of Christ, 300. Thence the *panagia* was celebrated also by later Greeks, 300. The sanctified bread placed in the *prothesis*, which was distributed to the people in place of holy communion, 300.
*Panni* opposite to *panni* are called *antipanina*, certain ornaments, 224, 268. *Panni* are tied to fragments called *epicambia*, 146. A covered table, which Greek bishops distributed, upon which it was permitted to sacrifice, 146.
*Pappias*, 12, 28. And the *megas pappias*, an office, 22. Palace, 10. Parasceve hours are celebrated as if floating [i.e., changing], 72, 326.
1232
Hunters of leopards enter the palace on horseback, 108. Parricide. Against him are the curses of the senate, 280. On Easter, the Emperor eats in the *triclinium*, 61, 76. During the office, a lamp is given to the Emperor by the protopapas, 73. Candles are given to the nobles and the podesta of the Genoese, 73. With closed doors, incense is offered, 73. The liturgy is sung in a low voice, 74, 76. The Emperor is honored with a kiss, 74. He hears vespers in St. Sophia, 76. Formerly, having entered the tabernacle, he would liberally give one hundred pounds of gold to the clergy as a gift, 77. On Easter it is permitted to use the *sakkos*, the patriarchal vestment, 169.
The patriarch consecrates water on the Epiphany, 65. He gives a lamp to the Emperor, ibid. He ordains deacons as his ministers and gives them the power of wearing the *phelonion*, 66. On the Thursday of Easter, together with the bishops, archimandrites, and the prefects of the churches, he visits the Emperor, 77. Having changed his habit, he honors the Emperor with incense and a kiss, and receives a kiss from the Emperor, 77. On a built platform, he performs the exaltation of the holy cross, 90. He carries him onto the ambo and reads the prayers composed for the anointing, 90. Anointing the head of the Emperor into the shape of the holy cross, he chants the *Sanctus*, ibid. With the Emperor's father, he places the crown on the head of the new Emperor, ibid. The Emperor takes the crown blessed by his hand to place on the Empress, ibid. They congratulate each other, 94. To both is chanted: "May the Lord remember," 95. Holding the holy chalice, he communicates the Emperor also with the holy Blood, 96. The Emperor kisses the hand of him as he blesses, ibid. For a future patriarch, three are designated, and the Emperor nominates one from their names brought to him, 102. Admission by suffrage is free to him, ibid. Being received in the *triclinium*, he is presented to the Emperor, 103. From him he receives the scepter by a certain formula, ibid. The elected one walks on foot outside the palace, but when announced, [he is brought] within the palace, 104. He is consecrated in the presence of the Emperor in St. Sophia by the Bishop of Heraclea, ibid. The Emperor sends letters to him under a leaden seal, 31. Through the invocation of the Holy Trinity, the Greek emperors promote patriarchs, 331. Phranzes describes the custom of announcing them more accurately, 373. Royal gifts and the formula of words, 373, 374. The patriarch elected by many was consecrated only by the Heraclean, except for Polyeuctus, [who was] ordained by the Caesarean, 371, 372. Other patriarchs were elected in the same way, 375. From the coronation of the Emperor, he made himself more austere in habit, 368. He signed with a blue color, adorned the head-covering with gold, and covered his ambition with piety, ibid. On the day of the catechesis for the Lenten fast, having exhorted the people, he distributed incense to them, 311. And so that he might make himself more graceful by giving, 332. He distributed *baia* and *rogai* on the day of the *Baiophoroi*, that is, Palm Sunday, 311. After some minor things he would give, such as candles and holy crosses, at Easter a three-headed [candle] with a kiss, 312. There was no peace between the patriarch and a schismatic, even with his schismatics, 373. The patriarch changed offices more often, 144, 162. However, it was not permitted to prefer some to others by *pittacia*, 164. His business was *apo-tika*, [i.e.] of the Emperor, [and] it was requested/granted, 164. Sacred vestments of the patriarch, 140, 141, 166, 169, 313, 379. The Patriarch of Alexandria alone used a miter or *infula* or *phrygium*, 169.
The prefect of the floor seems to have had the care of it, and one of the guardians of the sacred vessels, 161.
Dutch Calvinists admit the kissing of feet, 329. Taking it ill that a kiss is fixed to the foot of Christ's vicar, 328. The patriarch receives the holy scepter of feet from the Emperor, 105. Archimandrites, 105. Mahomet gave a gilded silver one to Gennadius, 372. The Greco-Roman law sets six *pentades* of offices, 162. Excellence of the first *pentade*, 118. No *syncelli* are included in the *pentade*, 377.
*Pentamaria*, that is, the fifth part of the spoils, went to the royal treasury, 255. That was not always observed, ibid. At Pentecost, the Emperor takes food in the *triclinium*, 64, 78. After lunch, he hears the prayers of St. Basil in St. Sophia, 78.
*Penula* (scarlet), that is, the *tamparium* of the despot, 13. It shines with a citrine color with *margellae* [borders/pearls?] for the *megas domestikos*, 17. It is wrongly called *phelone*, 167.
*Perea* or *Pera*, a region, 15. Included in the City, 122. Thence the Peratic monasteries of that region, ibid.
*Peripatos*, a procession, a word which seemed Calvinist to June, 315.
The Persian nation is small and pastoral, 53. Having abandoned their customs, Cyrus retained the name, ibid.
*Pescesium* or *bescesium*, that tax was called that which was imposed on a newly elected patriarch, i.e., two thousand florins, 373.
The Emperor visits the Monastery of Petra in prayer, 81.
*Phaceolia*, derived from the Medes, Cyrus uses, 54. The Emperor carries it, 50. Logothete of the course, 22. *Phaceolion*, a Turkish head-covering, the origin and writing of the word, 240
*Phalerae* are the trappings of the master of the horses, renewed at Easter with equestrian finery, 30. *Phelonion*, a sacred garment of the patriarch, bishops, and archdeacons, etc., without sleeves; it resembles the form of a sack, marked with many holy crosses, 166. Its mysteries and various spellings, ibid. The archdeacon puts it on with the epitrachilion on the feast of the Holy Cross, 65, 160. The patriarch performs its investiture, 66. It is not worn in Lent, except on the Annunciation, Palm Sunday, and Great Saturday, in a purple color, 67. The head of the philosophers held the dignity, 29, 184. *Phineus siromastes* was not a club, but either a javelin or a spear; the Vulgate calls it a dagger, 212 seqq. *Phrygium*, the mitre of the Patriarch of Alexandria, is said to have originated with St. Cyril, 169. *Phylacteria* of the Jews, 319. *Phytilia*, a portion of the booty due to the *protostrator*, 32. A sceptre with pictorial work for the grand interpreter, 25. *Pilaticia* worn by the *tzacones*, 37. A mark of judgment originated from the Medes, 54. The *protovestiarius* shows it three times at the Nativity, 49, 51. Pilei of the emperor and nobles at the Nativity, 47. Caesar Umbriatus, scarlet in color with holy crosses made of pearls, 11. Of the logothete of the treasury, white with pearls, 20. And the *clavatus* of the *megas domestikos*, 17. *Pincerna*, office, 14; palace rank, 9. Ornaments, 20. He serves the table, 55. Outside the city, he used to carry a cup by a small chain for the emperor’s sudden thirst, 58. From mixing (*pincendo*), named in Greek, ibid. Written *pincerna* and *epincerna*, but as one word, 184. The consul of the Pisans prays well for the emperor at the table, 57. *Pittacia* are short letters, 133. They originated with the Dorians, ibid. The *protonotarius* was in charge of preparing them, ibid. *Pocula* (cups) are on the bishop’s wavy *sticharion*, 166. Cups float on rivers, so that they might draw doctrine from both Testaments, ibid. Something similar regarding wavy *mandyae*, 379. Constantine Pogonatus, 323.
The chanters sing the *polychronion* to the emperor, 44. Then, with the *canonarch* leading, the bystanders acclaim, 46. Again, when the emperor is seen in the oratory, they sing it three times, 52. Also at dinner, 57, 15. Its perpetual repetition at the table by the nobles, soldiers, Anconitans, Genoese, Pisans, and the emperor’s reciprocal response, 55. *Polychronion* is shouted three times during the blessing, 79. When the emperor is sitting on his shield, 89. After the incense offering, 95. After Holy Communion while the emperors are sitting on the *anabathra*, 97. It is repeated three times at the creation of a *despotes*, 100. At the designation of a patriarch, it is repeated three times, 103. After entering, the first duty is that of the first of the deacons, 150. Another explanation of this matter, 151. And when the patriarch went out in public, the patriarchal domestics used to acclaim him this way, 163. Nothing is obtained from a Greek without the *polychronion*, 232, 278. *Polystaurion*, the patriarch’s garment, 41. Apples brought to the emperor’s table in carriages, 301. The images of sacred pontiffs are represented by a *flammula*, 48, 285. From where the *bullae* of pontiffs are named, 256. The Euxine Sea called *Maurothalassa*, 239. The people receive the *antidoron*, or sanctified bread; the bishop prays well, 365. He sings all night standing the hymn of the Mother of God, the *akathistos*, for the city preserved by her aid, 323. Clad in black (*pullatus*), he mourns with the emperor, 106. A pig smeared with mud jumping into the middle of the singers of sacred hymns, a sad omen of future confusion and faction, 340. *Porphyra*, the palace built by Constantine for empresses in labor, 302. Those born there were called *Porphyrogeniti*, ibid. At the porphyry column, a litany and divine office are held, at which the emperor is present, 79. Opposite it, the Caesar’s ambassadors are entertained, 339. *Postilenis* (cruppers) and *antilenis* (breastbands) of horses with a small *flammula*, 227. The right of postpone and antepone belongs to the emperor, 18. The patriarch also attempted the same, 144, 162.
The prefects of the regions led dances in the *Augusteum* in honor of the emperors, 355. Prefect of the *alogion*, office, 53; palace rank, 40. He is the *prefectus generalis* of the cavalry; the *princeps* of the cavalry is of higher rank, 200. The *prefectus antimensium* introduces the communicants, 5, 148. He has the care of the *antimensia* consecrated by the bishop, and those used in place of portable altars, 146. The *prefectus* of the basket and of the changing of patriarchal vestments is the ecclesiastical *vestiarius*, whose assistant is the *hieromnemon*, 136. The *prefectus* of the *contacia* begins the *contacia*, 139, 153. Prefect of the bedchamber or *cubicularius*, office, 17. Palace rank, 9. Insignia, 20. He is the head of the *cubicularii* and has under him the president of the bedchamber; he also carries the emperor’s sword, 34, 186, 191. The *prefectus* of the churches is the associate of the lowly *sacellarius*, 144. The *prefectus* of the gospel holds it in processions, 144. *Prefectus exercitus* (prefect of the army), office, 29. Palace rank, 10. His badges of office, 23. Ministry, 36. He goes before the army and searches for a place for the tents, 83. The *prefectus* of the lights takes care of the baptized, or the illuminated, 5. He differs from the *lampadarios*, 145. *Prefectus mensae* (prefect of the table), office, 21. Palace rank, 10. Insignia, 21. He serves the emperor’s table, 59, 60, 62, 63. The *prefectus* of the monasteries is an administrator under the great *sacellarius* in caring for them, 122, 124, 144. On the fifth day of Easter, he visits with the patriarch the emperor, 77. He kisses his cheek and hand, ibid. From the emperor, he receives the sceptre of the feet, 105. *Prefectus monocaballorum* or *stratopedarcha*, office, 67; palace rank, 12. Garments, 141. He used to supplicate the emperor on behalf of those afflicted by injustice, 141. *Prefectus sacellii* takes care of the catholic churches and the chapel, 122, 125. Especially the chapel and the money of the church, 124. The *prefectus* of the sacred station takes care of the order among the sacred ministers, 112. *Prefectus* of the seal or the signet ring, office, 16; palace rank, 9. Vestments, 20. He signs letters with the emperor’s *pala* or ring, 34. He was the keeper of the imperial ring, 185. In the absence of the *protostrator*, he used to hold the sword, 34. *Prefectus zangratorum*, office. *Praeses cubiculi* (president of the bedchamber), office, 60. Palace rank, 11. Badges of office, 26. He is in charge of the *cubicularii*, 41. *Praeses* of the great palaces, office, 69, and *praeses* in the Blachernae, office, 70; palace rank, 12. Insignia, 27. He takes care of these palaces by virtue of his office, 42. To which palaces were added more spacious ones before, with a temple built therein, 272, 582. *Praeses vestiarii*, office, 61. Palace rank, 11. Insignia, 26. He notes the accounts of receipts and expenses under the *protovestiarius*, 41. *Praesides* of cities, office, 79. Palace rank. The emperor’s bodyguard in the flourishing empire consisted of six thousand men, 48. *Praetor* of the people, office, 58. Palace rank, 10. Garments, 24. Lacks function, 39. A *prevaricator* is he who deserts his client and pleads for the opposing party, 311. He is not led from the *balis* or *baphius*, *Bato* against Balsamon, ibid. Justinian decreed that priests should not be under 60; Heraclius established it at 80, 112. *Primicerius* seems to be said by *paragoge* without a new meaning, 155. The *primiceriatus* is the office itself and the dignity of the *primicerius*, ibid. *Primicerius* of the palace, office, 33; palace rank, 10. Insignia, 23. He calls the nobles to the *circustatitio*, 31. In it he proposes imperial business to those who are absent, 35. In it he establishes the order among the courtiers, 36, 44. He again calls them to the *circustatitio* on the vigil of the Nativity, 56. He reports the joyful acclamation of the emperor to the greeters, and there for the third time calls them to the *circustatitio* while the emperor is reclining, 60. The *primicerii* of the Varangians receive dishes from the hand of the *megas domestikos*, 515. Of the Bardariotae, 38. *Primicerius* of the defenders in the Roman Church, the first among the *regionarii* defenders. Palace rank, 9. Garments, 19. Ministries, 33. He offers the emperor’s sceptre; once received, it is permitted to keep it for the sake of honor. On Sunday, he receives a *hyperpyron*. He is the head of the imperial band in the *fossatum*. He has his own *flammula*, ibid. 7, and *protalogator*, 40. He requests the emperor’s sceptre for the festive *circustatitiones* from a servant, 45. He offers it to the emperor on the vigil of the Nativity, 45. *Primicerius*, what is signified by this term, 155. *Primicerius* of the patriarchal notaries, 162. *Primicerius* of the tabularii is either the first of his catalog, or with respect to those whom he has below him, or he who presides over the archive as an associate of the *chartophylax*, 154. Or he is a notary himself, ibid. *Princeps* of the knights, superior to the *protalogator*, 200. *Princeps magnus*, office, 35; palace rank, 10. Ornaments, 23. The ministry is vacant, 39. When sending letters to great princes, he uses a leaden seal; the emperor uses other [ornaments/vestments] 15, to the *megas drungarios* of the *excubiae*, as he pleases; they wear *cabbadia* and *epilorica*, 22. The foot soldiers enter and leave the hall, 30. He gives them imperial letters under a leaden seal, 34. They are called to the *circustatitio*, 34. And under the table, 60. In the *circustatitio*, there is a fixed place for them, 37. In the oratory, the same order is kept as in the *triclinium*, 49. Their garments and insignia on the vigil of the Nativity, 47. At the table, they pray many years for the emperor, 55. The emperor prays the same for them, ibid. ... to take the *lapatza* with the belt and sleeves tied at the back and on the fifth day of Holy Week, 71. On Good Friday, 73.
And on the feast of Easter, 73. And of Pentecost, 77. During the office of the vigil of Preparation and the reading of the twelve Gospels, candles are given to them, 71. Some person of higher honor receives a lamp from the emperor at intervals, ibid. At Easter they again receive candles, 73. And they kiss the emperor’s cheek, hand, and right foot, 74. And there they are honored with the burning of incense, as is the patriarch, 76. And the bishops perform the standing in their place, 77. During the monthly blessing, before and after the joining of the emperor, they acclaim many years, 79. In the morning they gather at the highest point and lead the emperor to be crowned at Hagia Sophia, [page 1235 continues] 98. When the emperor ascends the ambo of the church, the nobles scatter coins, 98. Four shield-bearers stand by the emperor, who wears a crown, 99. The nobles adorn the *despotes* to be created, the *caesar*, and the *sebastocrator*, according to the emperor’s decree, 100, 101. They lead the candidate for the *despotate* to the emperor, 101. They acclaim many years, 100. In the renunciation of the patriarch, they stand outside the enclosure of the *triclinium*, 103. One introduces the patriarch to the emperor, ibid. They accompany the one renounced to Hagia Sophia, 104. In mourning, they wear black, dark, and blue garments according to the different customs of the funeral, 69, 106, 381. St. Procopius the martyr is represented by a small banner, 48, 281. The emperor’s relatives mourn in various ways and are mourned, 106. For nine days they remain at home, at night they approach the emperor and pay homage in black dress, afterwards they enter the hall in blue, 107. *Protalogator*, office: 54; palace rank: 11; habit: 25; carries an *inatzuca*, ibid. He takes care that a soldier does not flee under the great *primicerius*, and recalls a deserter, 40. He is the general prefect of cavalry, lesser than the prince of the cavalry, 200. *Protapostolarius*, first interpreter of the apostle, 143. Who publicly [reads] the apostle, 282. And on the Nativity he reads the apostle and the prophecy, 46. *Protecdicus* takes care of captives and the ecclesiastical court, 130. He moves a dispute about the seat to the *chartophylax*, 127. He is the first defender with respect to others, ibid. *Prothesis*, a place of the temple in which the holy things are stored, 93, 363. From there the procession begins, by which the material of the future sacrifice is carried to the altar, 94, 161. The emperor is called there to be crowned, 93. And outside the *prothesis* the emperor puts on the golden *mandya*, ibid. *Protocanonarcha*, 10. His true origin, function in starting a canon or a certain ode, 156. *Protonotarius*, an ecclesiastical official, at the gate of the *exocatacoeli* and the first after them, 132. The same was in charge of preparing the briefs, 133. *Protonotarius*, office: 57; in the palace he had notaries, 270. *Protopapas*, second from the patriarch, 6. And first among the common priests, but inferior to the *exocatacoeli*, 149. He is called the first of the priests and *protopapadas*, 150. He reads first in the vigil of the Nativity, the Gospel, 16. The *protopapas* of the palace blesses the emperor's table at the Nativity, 59. In the absence of the patriarch, he performs the consecration of the water at Epiphany, 65. He reads the Holy Gospel at the washing of the feet, 70. At his reading, he hands the lamp to the emperor, 71. Likewise at Easter, 73; in the same place, with doors closed, he burns incense and sings stichera, ibid. He performs the exaltation of the Holy Cross, 78. He places the Holy Cross on the emperor’s forehead, 79. He anoints his eyes and forehead with the material of sanctification, ibid. He is second from the *protopapa* and is the second of the priests, 6. It is not permitted for a *protopapas* to wear the *epigonation* and the *epimanicion*, 140. *Protopsaltes*, 6. His habit and office in the vigil of the Nativity, 44, 46. He carries an *anocheum* in the monthly blessing, at the *anabathra*; they sing as the emperor is crowned, *hastall*, 93. The church does not have a *protopsaltes*, but the emperor's choir does, 94; the exarch of the imperial choir, ibid. *Protosebastus*, the thirteenth dignity, green garments were taken from him, 8, 9. His other garments, 19. No office, 53. Also called *protosebastos*, count of the palace, and he obtained the fourteenth place, 183. The difference between *protosecretarii* and *secretarii* is that the former was the first secretary and a judge by palace dignity, while the other was merely an administrator, 141 seq. *Protopastharius*, office: 34; palace: 10. Amice: 23. No function, 38. First among the *spatharii*, 251. *Protostrator*, office: 8; palace: 9. His insignia, 18. He carries a sword, leads the emperor's horse, 29. He is the defender of those making excursions; from the booty he takes the *phytilia*; he is so called because he precedes the *arpatos* or the army, 32. He seems to be a marshal and is called the first of the equerries, 179. *Protosyncellus* and first among the companions of the cell, 376. Whence the name was made for the dignity, and in their absence he communicated, 377. Political dignity in Cedrenus, ibid. Concerning scripture, [page 1236 begins] *Protovestiarites*, office: 19. Palace: 9. Garments, 20. Ministry, 34. The minister of the standing calls the nobles, ibid. And in the Nativity, 44. He gives the signal for the standing to be ended, 35. In the same he proposes business to the emperor, ibid. He is different from the *protovestiarius*, 187. *Protovestiarius*, sixth palace dignity, 8. Green garments were transferred to this [office] by Michael Palaeologus, ibid. Prelate to the grand domestic and the *panhypersebastus* and again subjected, ibid. His insignia, 18. Ministries, 28. He ascends the oratory and shows the *pilaticia*, 49. By office he offers a crown or another sign of authority to the emperor, ibid. He removes dirt and insects from the emperor's garment, 50. A third time he removes the sublime *pilaticia*, the sign of the emperor’s presence, 51, 52. He is called and receives a dish from the emperor's table, 37. He was the first prefect of the *vestiarium*, 178. The archdeacon reads the psalms of the hours in the vigil of the Nativity, 46. Doctor of the psalter, 143.
Q
Quadragesima without meat, celebrated before the Nativity, 59. During this time the deacons did not wear purple *sticharia*, 67. The relatives mourn the deceased for forty days in black dress, 106. A square shield in the tent of the *sebastocrator*, 15. Of the *caesar*, 16. *Quaestor*, office: 45. Palace: 11. Insignia, 24. No function, 40.
R
Columns are covered with laurel, myrtle, or olive branches on Palm Sunday, 67. The flammula of the rulers of the people [is carried] behind the emperor and the nobles, 48. A referendary is sent to the emperor, 136. Following the coronation, he receives the *mandya* from the emperor, 93. Heraclius allowed twelve referendaries, 112. The kings of France carried the banner of St. Dionysius with them on expeditions, 286. The kings of the West and East washed the feet of the poor, 318. Regional defenders were established by St. Gregory, 131. The city of Constantinople is preserved by the relics of the saints, 322. The week of renewal or *diakainisimos*, 77. Said to be the paschal week, because all things are renewed by Christ rising, 353 sq. *Rhabduchi* also obtained dignity, 184. It is the work of the *Rhetor* (who seems to be a preacher) to interpret the Holy Scriptures, 144. *Rhomphaeae*, spears, and *rhomphaeocratores* are nobles standing near the emperor with shields, 99. Rite in the Nativity of serving the emperor’s table, 43, 55. Of rising from the table, 62. Rite interrupted of entering the tabernacle at Easter, and distributing gold to the clergy, 77. Diversity of rites in churches does not oppose the unity of the faith, 296. *Roga*, military stipend; *rogatores*, those distributing stipends, 260. *Roga*, gift of imperial and patriarchal liberality, 511. Strongly commended by Balsamon and ceased to be given, 331. Constantine consigned Rome to St. Silvester in honor of SS. Peter and Paul, 262. Roman affairs afflicted under Phocas, 321. Easterns and Westerns honor the Roman empire, 55. Romanus the deacon, of Emesa, author of the *contakia*, 139. *Rucharium* is the *vestiarium*, 25, 223. The emperor’s *spatha* or sword is kept there, 31. *Ruchum* or the *despote's* scarlet toga, 13. Also *rocus*, *rochetum*, 223. The emperor changes his *ruchum* at the vespers of the Nativity, 47.
S
On Holy Saturday laurel is scattered before the emperor, 72. And on the fifth week of Quadragesima the *acathistus* hymn to the Mother of God is celebrated, 321. *Saccus* is the imperial garment, 50. When being created, [the emperor] is dressed in it, 89. It differs from the *mandya*, 313. The *saccus* is also a patriarchal garment, ibid., 169. The *phelonion* has the form of a *saccus*, 167. It signifies the sack in which Christ was clothed for mockery, ibid. From *saccus* and *sacculus* (in its first and obvious meaning of a small bag) are derived *sacella*, *sacellum*, the great and small *sacellarius*, 121. *Sacellia* is a Syriac word for a receptacle, 125. *Sacellarius* the great, an office of the Church of Constantinople, also of the first pentad and among the *exocatacoeli*, 117. Called by the primary name from the *sacculus* or treasury of the church, whose...

Machramion, 188. Maeander, 7. Manomeni plain, 46, 57. Magedi, 4. Magnesia ad Sipylum, 7, 39. Grand Duke, 155. See also Lucas Notaras. His impious words, 148. After the city is taken, he deserts his station and is captured and held with his family, 166. Answers Mehemet, 169. Various accounts concerning him, ibid. Refuses to hand his son over to Mehemet, 171. His words to his sons before they were killed, 172. His wife dies, 177. Grand Master of Rhodes. See Rhodes. Mahomet. See Mehemet. Malachias, Metropolitan, 199. Mamaimi, 178. Mamalus, a castle of Caria, 45. Mantachia, 4, 58. His subjects desert Bajazet in battle, 35. Manuel Palaeologus. See Palaeologi. Mark of Ephesus, 119. Does not subscribe to the Florentine Decree, and why, ibid. and 120. B. Mary, image of the Hodegetria, see above under H. Mary, sister of the Emperor John, married to Gataluzo, 23. Mary, daughter of Alexius Comnenus, wife of John II, Emperor of Trebizond, 56. Dies, 120. Mary, daughter of Lazarus, wife of Bajazet, 6. Megademetrius, citadel, 159. Megra, 197. Mehemet I attempts in vain to liberate his father, crosses to oppose Musa, 51. Defeated, then victorious, 52. Mourns his brother Musa, ibid. Enters Adrianople, 53. Fulfills promises to Manuel, ibid. Brings Cüneyt into order, 57. Destroys the castle at Smyrna that the Rhodians were building, 59. His words to the Grand Master of Rhodes, ibid. Permits him to build a fortress in Caria, 60. Wages war on Naxos, ibid. Sends an army against the men of the new sect, 62, 65. Enters Lycaonia, 65. Conquers Mustafa and Cüneyt, ibid. Demands them back from Leontarius, 66. Provides funds for their support, 67. Wages war against the Wallachians, ibid. Stricken by apoplexy, 68. Dies, 69. What sort of man he was toward the Christians, ibid. Why he entrusted his son Murad to Amaseia, ibid. Harassed by Timur, 70. By his will, commands that his sons who are not yet of age be handed over to the Emperor Manuel, ibid. His death and his corpse are hidden, 73. Mehemet II is destined as successor to his father, 123. Marries the daughter of the Turkish ruler [Turcothris], 126. Goes to Magnesia, and returns to Thrace upon the announcement of his father's death, ibid. The Porte comes to meet him, 127. Proclaimed Emperor, ibid. Strangles his brother, 129. He himself was born of a bondwoman, ibid. Sends back the daughter of the Despot George, his stepmother, 130. Establishes peace with the Constantinopolitans, ibid. Pays them money for the support of Orhan, ibid. Hears the envoys of the princes, 131. Strikes a peace treaty with the Hungarians, ibid. Offended by the Greeks, he denies provisions to Orhan, 133. Prepares to build a new castle at the mouth of the Bosporus, ibid. To the envoys of Constantine...
with Murad, ibid. Also with Mehmed, 130. Never crowned emperor, 132. Complains through envoys about the new castle built by Mehmed, 134. Begs him that the suburban lands of Constantinople be preserved, 137. Sends envoys to Mehmed again, ibid. Seeks aid from the Pope and confirmation of the union, 141. Takes care that provisions be brought into the city, 144. Plans to defend the city together with the Venetians of Galata, 149. Does what he can, 150. Despairs of safety when the city walls are cast down, 156. Answers those urging him to surrender, 158. His lamentable words, 161. Is killed, ibid. By whom he was killed, 169. His head, being recognized, is brought to Mehmed and fixed to a column, ibid. Concerning him, 199. Demetrius, son of Manuel, Despot of Achaea, 75. His wife dies, 120. Is betrayed by the Albanians and defeated by Murad, 192, 200. John, Emperor, son of Emperor Andronicus the Younger, his character and war with Cantacuzenus, 8. Takes Helena Cantacuzena as his wife, 19. Gives himself over to sloth, 19, 22. Departs for Italy and returns, 20. Recovers Constantinople through the aid of Gattilusio, 21. Is imprisoned along with his sons Manuel and Theodore by his son Andronicus, who was blinded, and is liberated from there, 23. Becomes a tributary to Bayezid, 24. Fortifies the Golden Gate at Constantinople, ibid. Has it demolished by order of Bayezid, 25. He dies, ibid. John, son of Manuel, 29. His wives, 54. Is proclaimed Emperor of the Romans, 74. Administers the empire while his father is alive, 101. Has an equal standing with Murad, 109. And sends an envoy to him, ibid. Sends royal insignia to George the Despot, 116. Departs for Italy for the council, 118. Lands at Venice, 119. Returns to Constantinople, 120. Informs Murad about affairs in Italy, 120. Dies, 125. John, son of the blinded Andronicus, is blinded while still an infant, 23. Besieges Constantinople and is placed on the royal throne by Bayezid, 28. Goes to Lemnos and is declared king of Thessaly, 43. Concerning the same, 74. Lachanes or Lachynes, 73, 102. Michael, Emperor of the Palaeologi, 1, 5. Leases Phocaea and the alum tax to the Catanese, 90. Michael, son of Andronicus the Elder, 4. Manuel, Emperor, grandson of John, son of Andronicus the Younger, serves Bayezid in war, 24, 25. Flees his court, 23. Seeks aid from the Pope and other Christian princes, 26. Cedes the empire and leaves it to his nephew John, 29. His words, worthy of a Christian prince, ibid. Departs for the West, 42. Returns to Byzantium, 42. Enters into peace with the Muslim, 51. Sends an apocrisiarius to Mehmed, 53. Gives a wife to his son John, 54. Goes to the Peloponnese and appoints his son Theodore as Despot, 56. Speaks with Mehmed and returns to Constantinople, 56. Does not wish to betray Mustapha, who had fled to him, to Mehmed, 66, 243. Demands back from Murad, according to the testament of Mehmed, his underage brothers, 71. Arms against Murad, ibid. His sons by force, ibid. Thinks about a treaty with Murad, 87. Demands Callipolis and the sons of Mehmed, 89. As an old man, leaves the administration of the empire to his son John, 101. Sends to Murad, 102. Stirs up Mustapha against him. Theodore, son of Emperor John, brother of Manuel, Despot of Lacedaemon, 23. Theodore, son of Manuel, 29. Takes the daughter of Count Malatesta as his wife, 54. Despot of Lacedaemon, 74. Thomas, son of Manuel, 75. Flees to Italy, 192. A prophecy concerning the dynasty of the Palaeologi, 179. P Palavicinus the Persian, 108. Palda the Constable is beaten by Mehmed, 152. Panidus, 23, 43. Bread. Adjuration by bread, 70. To recite the name of the Pope from the sacred diptychs, 141. The custom of women in childbirth to receive the Eucharist, 146. Native land, 125, 199, 200. The generations of the Patriarchs, 2. Those professing voluntary poverty are killed in various places, 63. Pegsiari, 114. Peloponnese occupied by the Genoese, 4. The youth of the Peloponnese transported to Constantinople, 192. Percligia Mustapha, author of a new sect, 62. Fixed to a cross, 63. Pergamum, 4, 39, 46. Pergamus, 188. Perinthus, 224. Peritheorium castle, Murad cedes to Adorno, 100. Persians, 69, 70. Persia, 42. Perso-Turks, 69. Petronium, a castle in Caria, built by the Knights of Rhodes, 64. Libertini of St. Peter, ibid. Relics of St. Peter, Bishop of Nauplia, 199. Phalangia, 152. Phasis River, 193. Patma-Catoun, daughter of Bayezid, given as a hostage by her brother, 43, 54.
